About this text

The Noble Great Vehicle Sūtra on the Descent into Laṅkā, known in Sanskrit as the Āryalaṅkāvatāra Mahāyāna Sūtra and in Tibetan as Phakpa Langkar Shekpé Tekpa Chenpo'i Do, is one of the most influential scriptures of Mahāyāna Buddhism. It opens with the Buddha dwelling in the city of Laṅkā on the jewel-adorned summit of Mount Malaya by the ocean, surrounded by a great assembly of monks and of bodhisattvas gathered from many buddha fields, led by the bodhisattva Mahāmati. Through a long series of questions and answers between Mahāmati and the Buddha, the sūtra explores the nature of mind and perception, teaching that the appearances of the world arise from mind itself. It is especially celebrated for its treatment of the storehouse consciousness and of buddha nature, themes that shaped the Yogācāra tradition and later schools such as Chan and Zen. It is Toh 107 in the Kangyur, where it belongs to the general collection of Mahāyāna sūtras and is divided into several fascicles.

Translation

In the language of India: ārya laṅka avatāra mahāyāna sūtra. In the language of Tibet: The Noble Great Vehicle Sūtra on the Descent into Laṅkā. The first fascicle.

Homage to all buddhas and bodhisattvas.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One (the Buddha) was dwelling in the city of Laṅkā, on the summit of Mount Malaya by the ocean, which was adorned with all kinds of jewels and with various flowers. He was together with a great saṅgha of monks and with a great assembly of bodhisattvas, great beings, who had gathered from various buddha fields. They sported in many meditative absorptions, masteries, powers and superknowledges, and were headed by the bodhisattva Mahāmati. They had been empowered by the empowerment of the hands of all the buddhas, and were skilled in the meaning of fully understanding that the domain of experience is the appearance of one's own mind. They bore the semblances of different kinds suited to the various minds, forms and dispositions of sentient beings and to the ways of disciplining them. They had realized and penetrated the five dharmas, the natures, the consciousnesses, and the two kinds of selflessness.

At that time the Blessed One, having spent seven days in the abode of Sāgara, king of the nāgas, came forth, and was welcomed by Śakra (Indra), by Brahmā, and by many tens of millions of nāga maidens. He looked toward Mount Malaya in Laṅkā and thought: "Here on the summit of Mount Malaya in the city of Laṅkā, the tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly complete buddhas of the past also taught the Dharma which they cultivated: the wisdom that noble ones know individually within themselves, which is not the domain of the views of logicians, of non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. I too, in this very place, will set it forth and teach the Dharma for the sake of Rāvaṇa, lord of the yakṣas." With this thought, he smiled.

Then Rāvaṇa, lord of the yakṣas, through the blessing of the Tathāgata, heard this: that the Blessed One had come forth from the abode of Sāgara, king of the nāgas, surrounded and preceded by Śakra, by Brahmā, and by many hundreds of thousands of nāga maidens; that he had gazed upon the waves of the ocean, and, seeing how the consciousnesses that enter the ocean of the all-base consciousness are stirred by the wind of objects, and seeing the arising of their minds, he had uttered an inspired utterance.

Having heard this, he thought: "I shall go and request the Blessed One to enter Laṅkā. That will bring lasting benefit, help and happiness to me and to gods and humans."

Then Rāvaṇa, lord of the yakṣas, together with his retinue, entered a celestial palace of flowers and went to where the Blessed One was. When he arrived, he descended from the palace together with his retinue, and they loudly sounded drums and cymbals. They circumambulated the Blessed One three times. He took a vīṇā whose body was ornamented with sapphire, beryl and musāra stone, and from which hung as a fringe priceless ochre cloth like kapriyaṅgu. Having delighted the assembly, with the melodies ṣaḍjā, riṣabha, gāndhāra, dhaivata, niṣāda, madhyama and kauśika, and with many melodies blended together, harmonizing his voice with the strings and bringing it within the sound of the vīṇā, he sang in the melody of verse:

The very nature and way of mind, a treasury of Dharma,
Selfless, free from views, without stain,
The way of Dharma that reveals the knowing realized within oneself:
O Protector, please teach it here as well.

Your body truly amassed by virtuous qualities,
You, the Sugata, who display emanations through miraculous power,
Delighting in the Dharma that is realized within oneself,
O Sage, now is the time to enter Laṅkā.

Here in Laṅkā the Victors of the past
And their sons, bearing many forms, have dwelt.
O Protector, please expound the excellent Dharma here;
All the yakṣas of many forms will listen.

Thus Rāvaṇa, lord of Laṅkā, sang in the toṭaka meter, and then again he sang in the melody of verse:

When seven days had passed, the Blessed One
Came forth from the abode of the ocean's lord,
Within the ocean, home of sea monsters,
And afterward stayed upon its shore.

As soon as the Buddha had taken his seat,
Rāvaṇa, together with goddesses
And many kinds of yakṣas
Graced by Śuka and Sāraṇa,

Went by magic power along the way
To where the Protector was staying,
Came down from his flowery vehicle,
And honored and bowed to the Tathāgata.

Blessed by the Lord of Victors,
Not speaking of it openly to him, he said:
"I am Rāvaṇa, the Ten-Necked (Daśagrīva),
Lord of the rākṣasas, who have come here.

Please accept whoever lives in this city,
And accept my Laṅkā as well.
The perfect buddhas of the past also
Expounded the domain of self-awareness

In the middle of this city
Adorned with its jeweled peak.
Blessed One, you too, upon that same
Jewel-adorned peak,

Surrounded by the Victors' sons,
Please teach the stainless Dharma.
Those who dwell in Laṅkā and we ourselves
Wish to listen today.

Liberated from the ways of verbal explanation,
The domain of self-awareness,
The Sūtra of the Descent into Laṅkā,
Was taught by the buddhas of the past.

Surrounded by the Victors' sons,
The buddhas of the past as well,
I recall, explained this very sūtra.
Blessed One, please teach it.

The buddhas and the buddhas' sons
Who will appear in future times
Will also teach this magical way
On the jewel-adorned peak;
Out of compassion for the yakṣas
The Guides will teach it here.

This magical city of Laṅkā
Has delightful jewels of every kind,
Pleasing terraces and stone platforms,
And is adorned with canopies of jeweled nets.

Blessed One, here are yakṣas
Free of faulty yoga,
Who contemplate self-awareness,
Whom past buddhas have cared for,
Who have faith in the way of the Great Vehicle,
Who are devoted to it and lead others in.
There are yakṣiṇīs and yakṣa youths
Who wish to understand the Great Vehicle.

To Laṅkā, to Mount Malaya,
Teacher, Buddha, please come.
Kumbhakarṇa and the others,
The rākṣasas who dwell in the city,
Who rely upon the Great Vehicle,
Wish to hear of self-awareness.

I have done what is to be done for the buddhas,
And now too I do it fully.
Out of compassion for me,
Come to Laṅkā together with your sons.

My dwellings and the hosts of goddesses,
Necklaces of many kinds,
And the delightful Aśoka grove:
Great Sage, please accept them.

I act according to the command
Of those Victors' sons and of the buddhas.
There is nothing at all that I would not offer.
Great Sage, regard me with compassion.

When he had heard these words of his,
The Lord of the three realms of existence said:
"Lord of the yakṣas, the Protectors of the past,
Upon the jewel mountain,
Taught the teachings of self-awareness.
Regarding you too with compassion,
On the mountain adorned with jewels
Those of the future will also teach.
This is the abode of those endowed with yoga
Who practice the Dharma that is seen.
I and my sons as well
Should regard the lord of the yakṣas with compassion."

The Blessed One gave his consent and, without speaking,
Remained with a peaceful mind.
He took his seat upon the flower chariot
That Rāvaṇa had offered.

Then Rāvaṇa
And the other fearless Victors' sons as well,
Honored by goddesses with laughter and music,
Went to the city.

Having gone to that delightful city,
He received abundant offerings once more.
The host of yakṣas led by Rāvaṇa
And the yakṣiṇīs also made offerings.
The yakṣa daughters and yakṣa sons
Made offerings with nets of jewels.
Rāvaṇa too hung jewel-adorned necklaces
Upon the upper bodies
Of the Buddha, the Victor,
And of the Victors' sons.

The Blessed One, with the wise Victors' sons,
Having accepted the offerings presented,
Taught the Dharma
Of the domain of self-awareness.

The host of yakṣas and Rāvaṇa
Honored the best of speakers,
And greatly honoring Mahāmati,
Entreated him again and again:

"You are the one who asks all the buddhas
About the domain of self-awareness.
I, the yakṣas, and the Victors' sons
Wish to hear this.
The wise Victors' sons
And the yakṣas too entreat you.
You, great speaker among speakers,
Have entered the yoga even of yogins.
We respectfully entreat you:
Fearlessly ask about the way!

Free of the faults of the pratyekabuddhas,
The śrāvakas, and the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas),
The pure nature of reality that is self-awareness
Brings attainment of the buddha ground."

The jewel-adorned peaks,
And others too, adorned
With ten million jewels of divine substance,
The Blessed One emanated there,
And on each of the mountains the Protector
Displayed his own body.
On each of them Rāvaṇa
And the yakṣas were present.
The whole assembly, undiminished,
Appeared clearly on each one.

There all the fields
And whatever Guides were there,
And the lords of the rākṣasas there
Who dwell in Laṅkā, also appeared.
A Laṅkā rivaling that one,
Other Aśoka groves,
And those fine forests too
The Victor emanated.

On each of the Protector's mountains,
Urged on by Mahāmati,
Proclaiming self-awareness,
He taught the Dharma to the yakṣas.
The sūtras of a hundred thousand lines
Were taught in full upon the mountain.

Then right there the Teacher
And the Victors' sons vanished.
The yakṣa Rāvaṇa, seeing himself
Dwelling in his own house,
Reflected, thinking, "What was this?
Who taught it, and who heard it?
What was seen, and who saw it?
Where have those cities gone?
Those buddhas, those fields,
Those fine jewel forests, where are they?

Was this an illusion, or the play of a dream?
Or what is called a city of the gandharvas?
Was it a blur of the eyes, or a mirage?
Was it a dream, a barren woman's son?
What I saw here,
Was it a whirling firebrand, or smoke?
Or else, in the domain of the mind,
Was it the nature of reality of phenomena?

There is no view and no viewing,
No speaker and nothing to be spoken;
Yet the childish, intoxicated
By their own conceptions, do not know this.
Conceived otherwise, this conceptuality
Appears as if it were the Buddha and the Dharma.
Those who view things just as they appear
Do not see the Guide.
When conceptuality does not engage
And one does not see the Buddha,
If one then sees that non-engagement
Is the Buddha, that is the Buddha."

The lord of Laṅkā had undergone a thorough transformation of his basis, and the very moment he fully realized and understood that all is merely the appearance of his own mind, he abode in the stirring of conceptuality. Urged on by his former roots of virtue, with an intelligence skilled in all the treatises, he saw suchness just as it is. He did not depend on others. He was skilled in investigation by his own intelligence. He saw free of the views of the logicians. He did not rely on others. He was a yogin of the great yoga. He had realized and understood the great skill in means of bearing manifold forms. He was skilled in realizing the specific characteristics of all the grounds, higher and higher. He delighted in the solitude that is the intrinsic nature of mind, mentation, and mental consciousness. He saw the severing of the three continua. Since his intelligence had become free of causes and of the causes of all the non-Buddhists, he abode in the inner absorption into the tathāgatagarbha, the buddha ground. Then, through the intelligence of the buddhas, he heard a voice from the sky and from within: "Lord of Laṅkā, excellent, excellent! Again, lord of Laṅkā, you are excellent. As you train, so should yogins train."

"Just as you have seen, so should the tathāgatas and phenomena be viewed. If one views them in any other way, one will come to rest in annihilationism. Free from your mind, your mentation and your mental consciousness, and practicing inwardly, you should cultivate all phenomena, and not through attachment to views of external objects. You should not dwell in the realizations of the śrāvakas, the pratyekabuddhas and the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), nor in views and meditative absorptions that fall within the domain of the meaning of words. You should not delight in talk and tales, not view things in terms of the nature of the Vedas, not fall into the arrogance of royal power, and not meditate on the six meditative concentrations and the like. Lord of Laṅkā, this is the commitment of those who practice the Great Vehicle: the children of the Victor endowed with the yoga of great yogins, who utterly crush opponents, who dispel wrong views, who are skilled in turning back the view of a self, whose intellect is subtle and discerning, and who are skilled in thorough transformation.

"You must strive to understand entry into the ground of the Tathāgata's own personal realization. If you do so, Lord of Laṅkā, this path that you have embraced through the absorptions of one skilled in meditative absorption will be practiced again and again, ever higher and higher. It is not the domain of the approach followed by the śrāvakas, the pratyekabuddhas and the tīrthikas: as in the yoga of childish tīrthikas, the arising of attachment to the characteristic of appearing as the grasping of the yogin as a self; following after qualities and substances; clinging through attachment to views that have ignorance as their condition; being distracted by emptiness and by arising; attachment to conceptual thought; and imagining with an intention that has fallen into characteristics.

"Lord of Laṅkā, this understanding of the Great Vehicle, which brings about the realization of my own personal awareness, brings the attainment of various forms. One will also attain birth in distinctive states of existence. Lord of Laṅkā, this entry into the yoga of the Great Vehicle turns back the waves of the various consciousnesses, which are veils that cover; it does not fall into the state of the yoga of the tīrthikas. Lord of Laṅkā, the yoga of the tīrthikas arises from the tīrthikas' clinging to a self. Because they view with attachment the objects of the two natures of consciousness, this too of the tīrthikas is not yoga. Lord of Laṅkā, that is excellent. Seeing the Tathāgata, reflect upon that very meaning just as you have reflected upon it, and that itself is seeing the Tathāgata."

Then, on that occasion, Rāvaṇa thought: "Once again I would see in this way the Blessed One (the Buddha), master of all yogas, who turns back the yoga of the tīrthikas, who teaches the domain of personal awareness, who is free of illusions conjured by a conjurer, and whose mind has realized. For when yogins realize yoga, they come to understand the bliss of meditative absorption and the absorptions, and because they understand that, the yogins' term 'yoga' is designated as 'by this one understands.' Therefore I shall behold in this way the Compassionate One, who brings to an end the cause of the blaze of afflictions and conceptual thought, who is surrounded by the children of the Victor, who has entered the minds and intentions of all sentient beings, who is all-pervading and works toward omniscience, through a miraculous power that has turned away from characteristics. By seeing him, what has not been understood will be understood. And what I have understood will further increase, so that I attain the ground realized by the Tathāgata, who dwells in the blissful meditative absorption and the absorptions that engage in nonconceptuality."

Then at that time the Blessed One, knowing that the lord of Laṅkā had attained forbearance toward unborn phenomena, out of compassion for Daśagrīva, displayed himself on his mountain peak, adorned with many jewels and covered with jeweled nets, in just such beauty as before. And once again Daśagrīva, lord of Laṅkā, saw the beauty he had seen and experienced; on the peak, the body of the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, adorned with the thirty-two excellent major marks; on each mountain, his own body as well, seated before the tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly complete buddhas; the Tathāgata discoursing together with Mahāmati on the domain of personal awareness; surrounded by yakṣas, giving discourse in explanation; and also those fields together with their protectors.

Then at that time the Blessed One looked upon the assembly with the fleshly eye of a buddha, stretched himself like a lion king, and laughed a great laugh. From his ūrṇā hair, his sides, his thighs, his waist, his śrīvatsa and all the pores of his skin, rays of light poured forth. He blazed like the fire at the end of time; his splendor was like a rainbow; his maṇḍala of light blazed intensely like the rising sun. As Śakra (Indra), Brahmā and the world guardians looked on from the expanse of the sky, he, seated on a peak that rivaled the summit of Mount Meru, laughed a great laugh.

Then that assembly of bodhisattvas, and Śakra, Brahmā and the others, thought: "For what cause and what condition has the Blessed One, master of all phenomena, first smiling, laughed a great laugh and sent forth rays of light from his own body? And having sent them forth, he has settled his mind in the bliss of meditative absorption, the domain where the noble wisdom of his own personal awareness is seen; and without gazing at any sight, he has looked toward the directions with a lion's gaze, contemplating the course of Rāvaṇa's realization of yoga, and has fallen silent."

Then the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati, who had made his request earlier, felt compassion for Rāvaṇa. He knew the minds, the intentions and the conduct of that assembly of bodhisattvas, and he also looked toward the multitudes of people yet to come. He thought: "There are sentient beings who delight in reciting explanations, there are those who cling to sounds just as they sound, and there are all those who cling to the power of the yoga of the śrāvakas, the pratyekabuddhas and the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas). They will think, 'So even the Blessed Ones, the Tathāgatas, who have turned back from the domain of consciousness, burst into great laughter!' It would not do for their minds to become confused."

To dispel their astonishment, he asked the Blessed One, "Blessed One, for what cause and for what condition do you smile in this way?"

The Blessed One said, "Excellent, Mahāmati, excellent! And again, Mahāmati, you have looked at the nature of the world and at the wrong views of worldly beings, and understanding the minds of the three times, you have undertaken to question me. That is excellent of you, Mahāmati. In this way the wise should make it their nature to inquire thoroughly, for their own sake, for the sake of others, and for the sake of both.

"Mahāmati, this Rāvaṇa, lord of Laṅkā, asked two questions of the tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly complete buddhas of the past. Now he wishes to ask me too, in order to cultivate the characteristic of understanding the distinction within the two questions, something the yogis in the yoga of the śrāvakas, the pratyekabuddhas and the non-Buddhists have never experienced. This Daśagrīva, who wishes to ask in this way, will also put his questions to the Victors of the future."

Then the Blessed One, knowing this, spoke these words to the lord of Laṅkā: "Lord of Laṅkā, the Tathāgata grants you leave. You whose crown sways, ask me without delay whatever you wish. As I answer each of your questions, the ground of your conceptual thought will be thoroughly transformed. With an intellect skilled in and discerning the factors opposed to the grounds, you will examine, by way of personal self-awareness, the characteristics of the blissful abiding in samādhi. You will be embraced by the buddhas of samādhi, and you will abide in the bliss of peace. Passing beyond the faults of the samādhi of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, you will abide on the grounds called Immovable, Good Intelligence and Cloud of Dharma. Being thoroughly skilled in the reality, just as it is, of the selflessness of phenomena, you will receive empowerment from the Victors of samādhi upon a great jewel lotus spread out for you. On lotuses that accord with it you will bless your own body into many different forms, you will see your own body seated on those lotuses, and you will have each of them look into the face of the others. Thus that inconceivable domain is accomplished by one who is skilled in accomplishment, by such a one alone. Abiding on the ground of practice, by accomplishing an accomplishment embraced by skillful means, you will attain that inconceivable domain as well, and you will also attain the ground of a Tathāgata, transforming into a multitude of forms, never before seen by śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas, non-Buddhists, Brahmā, Indra or Upendra (Viṣṇu). Let your mind rejoice!"

Then the lord of Laṅkā, surrounded by his assembly together with his goddesses, having been granted leave by the Blessed One, rose from that jeweled peak, which through the rays of the jewel lotus was like a jewel lotus of stainless light. He conjured up flowers, garlands, incense, fragrant smoke, perfumed ointments, parasols, victory banners, divine pennants, necklaces, pearl strands, diadems and crest ornaments of many kinds, varied and distinct, together with other outstanding and supremely excellent kinds of ornament never seen or heard of before; drums and cymbals of gods, nāgas, yakṣas, rākṣasas, gandharvas, kinnaras and mahorogas, surpassing even those finer than human ones; and all the musical instruments belonging to the desire realm. He also emanated whatever special kinds of music are seen in other buddha fields. He covered the Blessed One and the bodhisattvas with a net of jewels. He raised up divine pennants of various cloths. He rose into the sky to the height of about seven palm trees. He sent down a rain from great clouds of offerings. He sounded drums and cymbals. Then, descending from the sky, he sat down on a second jeweled peak, adorned with a great jewel lotus like the light of the sun and of lightning.

Having sat down, he first smiled respectfully, and when the Blessed One had granted him the opportunity, he asked the Blessed One two questions:

"I questioned the tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly complete buddhas of the past as well, and they too gave answers. Now I ask the Blessed One also. Among the teachings that are spoken, this is one that the buddhas, and you as well, will surely explain. Blessed One, these two dharmas were taught by emanations conjured by magical power; they were not spoken by the tathāgatas in their essential nature. Blessed One, the tathāgatas in their essential nature teach the domain of the gateways of samādhi; they do not teach what has fallen into the conceptuality that is the domain of words. So, Blessed One, master of the Dharma, Tathāgata, arhat, perfectly complete Buddha, please explain those two dharmas well to me yourself. Blessed One, these sons of the Victors will also listen, and I too shall come to understand."

The Blessed One said, "Lord of Laṅkā, state those two dharmas!"

Then the lord of the rākṣasas, who wore on his body a diadem, armlets, a necklace and a garland of the finest diamonds, his body adorned with fine ornaments, said: "If even dharmas are to be abandoned, how much more so non-dharmas! Blessed One, how are these two dharmas to be abandoned? What are dharmas, and what are non-dharmas? These things are subject to abandonment as a pair. They fall within the characteristic of conceptuality. Their nature is conceptual, and they are neither the elements nor what is derived from the elements. Because the all-base consciousness is not fully known, they have no distinguishing characteristics. They abide with the nature of a tangle of hairs, and their objects belong to impure cognition, which comes to an end. How, then, are such natures to be abandoned?"

The Blessed One said: "Lord of Laṅkā, jars and the like are breakable by nature and subject to destruction, yet in the domain of the conceptuality of fools they appear as distinct particulars. Why do you not take this in the same way? If one adopts the individual conceptualizations of fools, then there is indeed a distinction between dharma and non-dharma. But it is not so for the seeing that leads to the realization of noble wisdom.

"Lord of Laṅkā, things such as jars, which fall into various characteristics, belong to fools; never mind that they do not belong to the noble ones. Consider blazing flames that are of a single nature and arise from a single blaze. In houses, dwellings, parks and buildings, depending on the wood, they appear with differences of long and short light and as distinct particulars. In the same way, why do you not take it that here too there is a distinction between dharma and non-dharma?

"And it is not only that the light radiating from a single continuum of flame appears as distinct particulars. Lord of Laṅkā, although they arise from a seed, there are the distinctions of sprout, shoot, stalk, node, leaf, branch, flower, fruit and awn. In the same way, for the outer and inner dharmas, which are of a nature to arise and which issue from ignorance, all that belongs to the aggregates, the elements and the sense sources, and all dharmas born in the three realms, there appear differences of pleasant form, of speech and of movement. And consciousness, though of a single character, as it engages in grasping objects, appears with distinctions of superior, middling and inferior, and as purified, as virtuous and as nonvirtuous.

"Lord of Laṅkā, it is not only that there are distinct particulars among those dharmas. If even on the yogic path of yogis who cultivate yoga there appear differences in the character of what each knows by personal awareness, then why would there be no difference between dharma and non-dharma, which arise from individual conceptualizations? There is indeed. Lord of Laṅkā, because of the characteristic of conceptuality, there is a distinction between dharma and non-dharma.

"Lord of Laṅkā, what, then, are dharmas? They are what is imagined by the conceptuality of non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas and fools, established from causes as qualities and substances; these are called dharmas. They are to be abandoned, to be let go; one should not individually imagine them as having characteristics. Because of attachment to dharmas that are the appearances of one's own mind, dharmas such as pots, which are imagined by fools and have never attained a body, do not exist. When one sees clearly in this way through insight, they are abandoned.

"Then what are non-dharmas? They are those that have not attained a self-nature. They are dharmas in which no conceptuality of characteristics moves, and which arise from the cause that is the object. When one sees that they do not enter into either the real or the unreal, one therefore abandons non-dharmas.

"Again, what are the dharmas that have not attained a self-nature? They are dharmas such as the horn of a rabbit, of a camel, of a donkey or of a horse, and the son of a barren woman. Because they have not attained a self-nature, they should not be imagined as having characteristics. Otherwise, they are spoken of for the sake of convention, but not with attachment, as with jars and the like. Just as these are to be abandoned because consciousness cannot grasp them, so too the entities of conceptuality are to be abandoned. Therefore dharma and non-dharma are abandoned. Lord of Laṅkā, what you asked, 'How are the two, dharma and non-dharma, to be abandoned?', has been explained in this way.

"And as for what you said, Lord of Laṅkā, 'I asked the former tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly complete buddhas as well, and they too answered me,' Lord of Laṅkā, this word 'former' is a conceptual designation. Even the past is conceived of in this way as 'past.' The future and the present are conceived of likewise. The tathāgatas, by their very nature, are nonconceptual; they have passed beyond all the elaborations of conceptuality. They are not as one imagines the essential nature of form. Rather, for the sake of the ease of realizing wisdom, they meditate with a discernment that engages in the signless. Therefore the tathāgatas have wisdom as their very self; they are the body of wisdom, and they are not to be imagined.

"By what are they not to be imagined? They are not to be imagined by the mind as a self, a life or a person. How are they not to be imagined? They are not to be imagined by the mental consciousness, which arises from the cause that is the object, as one imagines the characteristics, shapes and aspects of form. Therefore you should be free of what is to be imagined and of conceptuality.

"Lord of Laṅkā, moreover, the activities of beings are like figures painted on a wall. Lord of Laṅkā, because no dharma exists, the abiding of the world is without movement, free of action and agent. In it there is no one who listens and nothing to be heard. Lord of Laṅkā, though the abiding of the world is like a magical emanation, the foolish yogis of the non-Buddhists do not cultivate this."

Lord of Laṅkā, whoever sees in this way sees correctly. If one sees in any other way, one is engaged in conceptual thought. Those who have conceptual thought in this way grasp things dualistically. It is like the reflection of one's own form in a mirror, the shadow of one's own body in water, moonlight, the shadow of one's own body cast by a lamp burning in a house, or an echo. Moreover, they firmly grasp what their own conceptual thought has grasped and conceive of dharma and non-dharma. They do not engage in abandoning both dharma and non-dharma. Instead they engage in them, they conceptualize, they proliferate, and they do not attain peace. This is a synonym for one-pointedness. It is the arising of the supreme samādhi, which leads into the tathāgatagarbha, the domain of the noble wisdom that each realizes within himself.

This is the first chapter, on Rāvaṇa's request.

Then the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati, who travels to all the buddha-fields, rose from his seat through the power of the Buddha, together with the bodhisattva Mati. He draped his upper robe over one shoulder, placed his right knee on the ground, joined his palms toward the Blessed One (the Buddha), and bowed. Then he praised the Blessed One with these verses:

The world is free of arising and perishing,
Much like a flower in the sky.
Through your wisdom and compassion
You apprehend neither existence nor nonexistence.

All phenomena are like illusions,
Free of mind and consciousness.
Through your wisdom and compassion
You apprehend neither existence nor nonexistence.

The world, free of permanence and annihilation,
Is ever like a dream.
Through your wisdom and compassion
You apprehend neither existence nor nonexistence.

The selflessness of phenomena and of persons,
Your afflictions and the knowable,
Through your wisdom and compassion
And through signlessness, are ever pure.

How can one praise the Dharma body,
Whose nature is illusion and dream?
The non-arising of things that lack intrinsic nature,
That itself is praise.

He whose seeing is free of senses and objects
And without appearance:
Whether one praises him or reviles him,
What can be said of that Sage?

You do not pass into nirvāṇa, nor are you in it;
Nirvāṇa does not abide in you.
Free of knower and of known,
You have abandoned the positions of existence and nonexistence.

Whoever sees the Sage at peace,
Thus free of arising and perishing,
Here and hereafter as well,
Will be free of attachment and free of grasping.

Then the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati praised the Blessed One with these fitting verses, and announced his name and family to the Blessed One:

Blessed One, I am Mahāmati,
Who has come to understand the Great Vehicle.
One hundred and eight questions
I shall put to the supreme of speakers.

When he had heard those words,
The Buddha, supreme knower of the world,
Surveyed the whole assembly
And spoke to the son of the Sugata:

"Ask me, children of the Victor!
Mahāmati, you too, ask!
The domain of individual self-awareness
I shall explain to you."

Then, the Blessed One having given him the opportunity, the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati touched the Blessed One's feet and asked the Blessed One:

How does reasoning become purified?

In what way is there error?
For what reason does error arise?

Lineage and non-lineage, how and through what?

What makes one free of conceptual thought?

Protector, how many kinds of wisdom are there,

The variety of beings and things,
And their designations, who produced them?

And the sun and moon, what are they like?
How many kinds of liberation are there?
How many kinds of yogins are there?

And how does one become a teacher?
How many kinds of buddhas are there?

And how many kinds of heterodox ones?

How many kinds of drawings are there?
And what is mere designation like?
Best of speakers, tell me!
From what do the clouds and winds of the sky arise?

Lord of the three realms of existence, tell me!
Why is it that elephants, deer,
and dull horses can be caught?
From what do base people arise?
You who guide the mind, tell me!
Why are the six elements grasped?
And how does one become an icchantika?
How are men, women,
and those of neither sex born? Tell me!
How does one turn back from yoga?
And how does one enter into yoga?
In what kind of person, and in what way,
is yoga established? Tell me!
Of beings who have gone into the destinies,
what are the characteristics and the signs?
By what, and how, does one become wealthy?
You who are like space, tell me!
How and why did the Śākya clan
and the Ikṣvākus come to be?
Who was the sage of long austerities,
and how did he instruct you?
Why are you, in all the fields,
with various names and likewise forms,
surrounded by the Victor's heirs,
present everywhere?
How is it that meats are not fit to eat?

And how do those born
in the lineage of flesh-eaters come to eat meat?
Why are the fields shaped like the sun and moon,
like Mount Meru and the lotus,
and shaped like the śrīvatsa and the lion?
Tell me why!
Those lying crosswise and those upside down,
and those like illusions, what are they like?
And the fields made of every jewel,

Those shaped like lutes and drums,

Some are emanation buddhas,

For what purpose, in Akaniṣṭha,

After the Sugata has passed into nirvāṇa,
who will hold the teaching?
How long does the Teacher remain?
And how long will the system endure?
How many kinds of tenets are yours?
And how many kinds of views?
The true monk and the Vinaya too,
why and how are they? Tell me!
Of pratyekabuddhas, the Victor's heirs, and śrāvakas,
why is there the king of transformation,
and the king of the imageless too?
Tell me why!
By what do the worldly superknowledges arise?
And how the transcendent ones as well?
And mind and the seven grounds,
how and why are they? Tell me!
How many kinds of Saṅgha are yours?
How does schism in the Saṅgha come about?
And the treatises for healing beings,
how and why are they? Tell me!
"I am Kāśyapa, Krakucchanda,
and Kanakamuni as well":
why, Great Sage, do you say this
to the Victor's heirs?
Why do you proclaim no self and no being?
How is there talk of permanence and destruction?
Why do you not teach everywhere
that mind-only is the very truth?
How are the groves of men and maidens,
the groves of harītakī and āmalakī,
the snows of Kailāsa and the Cakravāla mountains,
and the vajra-hard as well?
Within them, the many mountains,
richly adorned with various jewels
and filled with sages and gandharvas,
how and why are they? Tell me!

When the Buddha, foremost of those who know the world,
the great god, heard this,
the supreme heart of the buddhas,
the many ways of the Great Vehicle,
he said: "You of great wisdom, excellent, excellent!
All that you have asked
I shall explain in order.
Great-minded one, learn from me.
Birth and non-birth,
how nirvāṇa is, and emptiness,
transmigration and the absence of intrinsic nature,
buddhas, the perfections, and the heirs,
śrāvakas and the Victor's heirs,
non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) who practice the formless,
Mount Meru, the oceans and the mountains,
continents, lands and fields,
stars, sun and moon,
tīrthikas, gods and asuras,
liberations, masteries and superknowledges,
powers, dhyānas and samādhis,
cessation and the bases of miraculous power,
the factors of awakening and the paths,
the immeasurables and the dhyānas,
the aggregates, going and coming,
the meditative attainments and cessation,
emergence and the various explanations,
mind, mentation and consciousness,
selflessness and the five dharmas,
intrinsic nature, conceptualizing and the conceptualized,
appearance and the two views, how they are,
the source of the vehicles,
the mines where gold, gems and pearls arise,
icchantikas and the great elements,
the one buddha and the tumult,
knowing and the knowable, arising and attainment,
the possible and the impossible for beings,
why elephants, deer and horses
can be caught and how: this you asked me.
How is the exposition of tenets
endowed with examples and reasons?
Why are there cause and effect?
Methods and the various errors,
mind-only, which is imageless,
the absence of sequence in the grounds as well,
imagelessness and the transformations:
why these hundred? This you asked me.
The treatises of healing and the crafts,
the arts and the sciences likewise,
the mountains, likewise Mount Meru,
and what the measure of the earth is,
the measure of sun, moon and ocean,
how great it is: this you asked me.
In the bodies of beings, how many particles,
of the superior, the middling and the inferior?
In each field, how many particles?
In each fathom, how many are there?
In a cubit, a fathom, a pace, a krośa,
a half yojana and a yojana,
how many rabbit-hair motes, sunbeam motes, nits,
sheep-hair motes, and barley grains?
In a full measure, how many barley grains?
In a half measure, how many barley grains?
Likewise in one load of measures,
in a hundred thousand, ten million and a shudok, how many?
In a mustard seed, how many atoms?
In a peppercorn, how many mustard seeds?
In one ri, how many peppercorns?
In one nam, how many ri are there?
In one zho, how many nam are there?
In one sang, how many zho are there?
With such heads of reckoning as these,
how many sang are there in Mount Meru?

Thus my son has asked me.
Why do you ask in another way?
Why do you not ask in this way:
"How many atoms are there in the bodies
Of the pratyekabuddhas, the śrāvakas,
The buddhas and the children of the Victor?"
How many atoms are there in a tongue of flame?
How many atoms in the wind?
How many atoms in each single sense faculty?
How many pores in the hair of the eyebrows?
From what do wealthy people come to be?
And the wheel-turning kings,
How do they guard their kingdoms?
How do they become liberated?
How does one speak in single verses?
Sexual union, the various kinds of beings,
The many kinds of food and drink,
How it is that persons are young,
How and why the mountains
Are hard like vajra, tell me.
Why are things like illusions and dreams?
In what way are they like a mirage?
Where is it that clouds arise?
From what do the seasons arise?
From what does the savor of flavors come?
From what do male, female and neuter come?
The beauty of the children of the Victor,
Why, my son, do you ask me of these?
How are the mountains of the gods
Adorned with sages and gandharvas?
Where does the liberated one go?
Who is bound, and by what is one freed?
What is the domain of the meditators?
Emanations and also the tīrthikas,
Existence, nonexistence, non-doing, why are these?
How does one turn back from appearances?
How do the logicians cease?
Why does logic arise?
Through what does action arise?
Why do you speak to me of the going of beings?
By what is perception to be cut off?
Through what is meditative absorption realized?
Who is it that abides having conquered the three realms of existence?
What is the abode, and what the body?
It is said there is no self and no sentient being;
In what way is the conventional taught?
Why do you ask about characteristics?
In what way do you ask about selflessness?
Why, about the essence and the classes,
Do you ask me, child of the Victor?
Views of permanence and annihilation,
And by what the mind is settled in equipoise,
Speech, and likewise wisdom,
Moral discipline, lineage, child of the Victor,
Teacher and disciple, and the showing of reasoning,
In what way sentient beings are various,
Food and drink, space and the intelligent,
The māras and mere designation,
Trees and creepers, why these,
Why, child of the Victor, do you ask me?
Why are the fields various?
The austerities of the long-lived sages,
And what is your lineage and your teacher,
Why, child of the Victor, do you ask me?
Why is it that one who is lowly among humans
Does not awaken in the desire realm?
The culmination attained in Akaniṣṭha,
How it is reasoned, you ask me.
Why are there worldly supernormal knowledges?
How does one become a monk in actuality?
About the emanated buddha and the buddha abiding in ripening,
In what way do you ask me?
The buddhas of true wisdom,
And the Saṅgha too, how do they come to be?
Like a lute, a flower, a drum,
And the fields free of appearances,
The mind, and the seven grounds,
These too, child of the Victor, you ask me.
These, and very many others besides,
The questions my son has asked me,
Each one endowed with its marks,
And free of the faults of wrong views,
The established conclusion on the side of explanation,
I shall explain; listen to me.
I shall set forth the statements,
The hundred and eight statements,
Just as the buddhas have explained them;
Listen to me, my son.

Then the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, what are the hundred and eight statements?"

The Blessed One said: "The statement of arising and the statement of non-arising; the statement of permanence and the statement of impermanence; the statement of characteristics and the statement of no characteristics; the statement of transformation and the statement of non-transformation; the statement of the momentary and the statement of the non-momentary; the statement of intrinsic nature and the statement of no intrinsic nature; the statement of emptiness and the statement of non-emptiness; the statement of annihilation and the statement of non-annihilation; the statement of mind and the statement of no mind; the statement of the Middle Way and the statement of what is not the Middle Way; the statement of the everlasting and the statement of the non-everlasting; the statement of conditions and the statement of non-conditions; the statement of causes and the statement of no causes; the statement of afflictions and the statement of no afflictions; the statement of craving and the statement of no craving; the statement of skillful means and the statement of non-means; the statement of skill and the statement of non-skill; the statement of purity and the statement of impurity; the statement of reasoning and the statement of non-reasoning; the statement of example and the statement of no example; the statement of the disciple and the statement of the non-disciple; the statement of the teacher and the statement of the non-teacher; the statement of lineage and the statement of no lineage; the statement of the three vehicles and the statement of no three vehicles; the statement of appearance and the statement of no appearance; the statement of aspiration and the statement of no aspiration; the statement of the three spheres and the statement of no three spheres; the statement of signs and the statement of no signs; the statement of the position of existence and nonexistence and the statement of what is not the position of existence and nonexistence; the statement of both and the statement of neither; the statement of the personally realized wisdom of the noble ones and the statement of no personally realized wisdom of the noble ones; the statement of happiness in this very life and the statement of no happiness in this very life; the statement of fields and the statement of no fields; the statement of atoms and the statement of no atoms; the statement of water and the statement of no water; the statement of delusion and the statement of non-delusion; the statement of the real and the statement of the unreal; the statement of number and calculation and the statement of no number and calculation; the statement of supernormal knowledge and the statement of no supernormal knowledge; the statement of weariness and the statement of no weariness; the statement of density and the statement of non-density; the statement of the arts, sciences and crafts and the statement of no arts, sciences and crafts; the statement of wind and the statement of no wind; the statement of earth and the statement of no earth; the statement of mind and the statement of no mind; the statement of designation and the statement of no designation; the statement of intrinsic nature and the statement of no intrinsic nature; the statement of the aggregates and the statement of no aggregates; the statement of sentient beings and the statement of no sentient beings; the statement of intellect and the statement of no intellect; the statement of nirvāṇa and the statement of no nirvāṇa; the statement of the knowable and the statement of no knowable; the statement of the tīrthikas and the statement of no tīrthikas; the statement of turmoil and the statement of non-turmoil;

the statement of illusion and the statement of no illusion, the statement of dream and the statement of no dream, the statement of mirage and the statement of no mirage, the statement of reflection and the statement of no reflection, the statement of the wheel and the statement of no wheel, the statement of gandharvas and the statement of non-gandharvas, the statement of gods and the statement of non-gods, the statement of food and drink and the statement of no food and drink, the statement of sexual union and the statement of no sexual union, the statement of views and the statement of no views, the statement of the perfections and the statement of no perfections, the statement of moral discipline and the statement of no moral discipline, the statement of sun, moon and stars and the statement of no sun, moon and stars, the statement of truth and the statement of no truth, the statement of the fruit and the statement of no fruit, the statement of cessation and the statement of no cessation, the statement of emergence from cessation and the statement of no emergence from cessation, the statement of healing and the statement of no healing, the statement of the marks and the statement of no marks, the statement of limbs and the statement of no limbs, the statement of knowledge of the arts and crafts and the statement of no knowledge of the arts and crafts, the statement of meditative concentration and the statement of no meditative concentration, the statement of error and the statement of no error, the statement of appearance and the statement of no appearance, the statement of protection and the statement of no protection, the statement of lineage and the statement of no lineage, the statement of sages and the statement of non-sages, the statement of kingship and the statement of non-kingship, the statement of the apprehended and the statement of nothing apprehended, the statement of jewels and the statement of no jewels, the statement of prophecy and the statement of no prophecy, the statement of icchantikas and the statement of no icchantikas, the statement of male, female and neuter and the statement of no male, female and neuter, the statement of taste and the statement of no taste, the statement of action and the statement of no action, the statement of the body and the statement of no body, the statement of reasoning and the statement of no reasoning, the statement of movement and the statement of no movement, the statement of the sense faculties and the statement of no sense faculties, the statement of the conditioned and the statement of the unconditioned, the statement of cause and effect and the statement of no cause and effect, the statement of the lowest and the statement of Akaniṣṭha, the statement of time and the statement of no time, the statement of trees, shrubs and spreading creepers and the statement of no trees, shrubs and spreading creepers, the statement of diversity and the statement of no diversity, the statement of introduction through teaching and the statement of no introduction through teaching, the statement of Vinaya and the statement of no Vinaya, the statement of monks and the statement of no monks, the statement of blessing and the statement of no blessing, and the statement of letters and the statement of no letters. These, Mahāmati, are the one hundred and eight statements spoken by the buddhas of the past.

The Noble Descent into Laṅkā, second fascicle.

Then again the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, of how many kinds are the arising, abiding and cessation of consciousnesses?"

The Blessed One said: "Mahāmati, the arising, abiding and cessation of consciousnesses are of two kinds, which the logicians do not understand: namely, cessation of the continuum and cessation of the characteristic. The arising of consciousnesses is likewise of two kinds: arising of the continuum and arising of the characteristic. Abiding too is of two kinds: abiding of the continuum and abiding of the characteristic.

"Mahāmati, consciousness has three aspects: the characteristic of functioning, the characteristic of karma, and the characteristic of its own kind. Mahāmati, the consciousness that is taught as having eight characteristics is, in brief, of two kinds: the appearing consciousness and the consciousness that discriminates entities one by one. Mahāmati, just as forms appear in a mirror, so the appearing consciousness takes hold of what appears. Mahāmati, these two, the appearing consciousness and the consciousness that discriminates entities one by one, are not different in character, and each is the cause of the other.

"Mahāmati, of these, the appearing consciousness arises from the cause of the transformation of inconceivable habitual tendencies. Mahāmati, the consciousness that discriminates entities one by one arises from the cause of conceptualizing objects and from the cause of the habitual tendencies of beginningless conceptual elaboration.

"Mahāmati, the cessation of all the sense consciousnesses is this: the cessation of the various habitual tendencies of false imagination in the all-base consciousness. Mahāmati, this is the cessation of the characteristic. Mahāmati, the cessation of the continuum concerns that from which it arises. Mahāmati, 'that from which' means that on which it rests and that on which it is focused. Here, 'that on which it rests' means the habitual tendencies of the negative states of beginningless conceptual elaboration. 'That on which it is focused' means conceptualizing the appearances of one's own mind as the objects of consciousness.

"Mahāmati, it is like this. For example, the atoms of clay and a lump of clay are neither one nor different, just as gold and its ornaments are. Mahāmati, if the lump of clay were something different from the atoms of clay, it would not be made from them; but since it is made from those very atoms of clay, it is therefore not different. Yet if they were not different, there would be no distinction between the lump of clay and the atoms. Likewise, Mahāmati, if the functioning consciousnesses were separate and different from the characteristic of the all-base consciousness's own kind, they would not arise from the all-base consciousness as their cause.

If, however, they were not different, then when the evolving consciousnesses ceased, the all-base consciousness would cease as well. Yet its own class-characteristic does not cease in that way. Therefore, Mahāmati, the consciousnesses' own class-characteristic does not cease; what ceases is the characteristic of their activity. If their own class-characteristic ceased, the all-base consciousness would cease as well. And, Mahāmati, if the all-base consciousness ceased, this doctrine would be no different from the annihilationist doctrine of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas).

Mahāmati, the doctrine of the tīrthikas is this: when the grasping of objects is cut off, the continuum of consciousness is cut off; and when the continuum of consciousness is cut off, the continuum of beginningless time is cut off as well. Mahāmati, the tīrthikas assert that the continuum arises from a cause. They do not assert that eye consciousness arises from the coming together of form and light apart from that cause. Mahāmati, the cause they speak of is the principal (pradhāna), the person (puruṣa), the Lord (Īśvara), time, or atoms.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, there are seven kinds of intrinsic nature of things: the intrinsic nature of collection, the intrinsic nature of existents, the intrinsic nature of characteristics, the intrinsic nature of the great elements, the intrinsic nature of causes, the intrinsic nature of conditions, and the intrinsic nature of accomplishment. These are the seven.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, there are seven kinds of ultimate: the domain of mind, the domain of wisdom, the domain of the two views, the domain beyond the two views, the appearance-less domain, the domain where the sons of the buddhas pass beyond, and the domain of the Tathāgatas' individual self-awareness. These, Mahāmati, are the intrinsic nature of things and the heart of the ultimate of the tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly complete buddhas of the past, future and present. Because they possess that intrinsic nature of things and that heart of the ultimate, the tathāgatas, with the eye of noble wisdom, set forth the supreme dharmas, worldly and transcendent, that belong to specific and general characteristics; and they set them forth in such a way that they are not held in common with the doctrines of the tīrthikas and with wrong views.

Mahāmati, if one asks how they are not held in common with the doctrines of the tīrthikas and with wrong views, it is like this. Mahāmati, because they do not understand the view that conceptualizes the consciousnesses' own domain, and do not enter into the fact that it is merely the appearance of their own mind, ordinary childish beings come to speak dualistically, taking things and the nature of non-things as ultimate.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, I shall explain the cessation of the conditions of ignorance, craving and karma; the reversal of the conceptualized suffering of the three realms of existence; and the seeing of the domain that appears to one's own mind as an illusion.

Mahāmati, certain śramaṇas or brahmins hold that a result becomes manifest from a cause, out of what has not come to be or out of what exists; that things endure; that from conditions the aggregates, elements and sense sources arise and abide, and having arisen, perish. Mahāmati, they come to view continuity, activity, birth, existence, nirvāṇa, the path, karma and its result, and the truths as destroyed and annihilated. And why is that? Because no beginning is seen, since it is neither directly perceived nor apprehended.

Mahāmati, it is like this: a potsherd does not perform the function of a pot, for the pot is no more. Nor does a burnt seed perform the function of a sprout. Likewise, Mahāmati, those things, the aggregates, elements and sense sources, that have ceased, are ceasing and will cease, arise from the cause of seeing the conceptualizations that appear to one's own mind; therefore they do not arise in an unbroken continuum.

Mahāmati, if the three kinds of consciousness were to arise, out of what has not come to be or out of what exists, by virtue of the function of the condition that is the meeting of the three, then, Mahāmati, nonexistent things such as a tortoise's hair would arise, and sesame oil would come even from sand. Mahāmati, for those who assert arising from the existent and the nonexistent, their thesis collapses, they contradict what is certain, and action, karma and agent also become meaningless.

Mahāmati, they too, because they hold to the function of the condition that is the meeting of the three, teach cause and result and specific characteristics. They take the characteristics of existence and nonexistence to be present in what is past, future and presently arisen; and abiding on the ground imagined by their treatises, they teach under the influence of the habitual tendencies of their own wrong views. Thus, Mahāmati, ordinary childish beings, seized by wrong views and of uneven understanding, out of ignorance say of what they teach, "This was taught by the Omniscient One."

Mahāmati, certain śramaṇas or brahmins see things as without intrinsic nature, like clouds, a whirling firebrand, a city of gandharvas, the unborn, an illusion, a mirage, the moon in water, and a dream; they see the conceptualization of what appears to the mind as external, and the proliferation of beginningless time. By so seeing, they will come to meditate upon the arising of their own mind: the appearance of their own mind, in which the conditions for conceptuality do not return; free from imagined expressions, from characteristics and the bases of characteristics, and from what is to be expressed; free from the all-base consciousness that appears as body, enjoyments and abode, from objects, and from apprehended and apprehender; the appearance-less domain; that which has abandoned arising, abiding and destruction. Mahāmati, those bodhisattva great beings will before long attain the sameness of saṃsāra and nirvāṇa.

Mahāmati, there are those whose practice has made great compassion and skill in means spontaneously present. They regard all the realms of sentient beings as equal to illusions and reflections, as not brought about by conditions, and as empty of outer and inner objects. Because they view nothing as lying outside the mind, they are endowed with the blessing of signlessness. They gradually enter the domain of samādhi of the successive grounds. By meditating thoroughly, with deep conviction that the three realms are an illusion of their own mind, they will attain the illusion-like samādhi.

By entering into the mere nonappearance of their own mind, the attainment of abiding in the perfection of wisdom arises. Bodhisattvas endowed with that activity will then attain the body of a Tathāgata. That body is like the vajra-form samādhi and accords with the body of the Tathāgata. It is endowed with the emanations of suchness, and adorned with the powers, the superknowledges, the masteries, loving-kindness, compassion and skillful means. It goes even to the abodes of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) in all buddha fields. It is free of mind, mentation and mental consciousness, and it is attained through the gradual transformation of the basis.

Therefore, Mahāmati, bodhisattva mahāsattvas who attain conformity with the body of the Tathāgata should follow mind-only. They should remain free of the proliferation of concepts about aggregates, elements and sense bases; about mind, causes, conditions and activity; and about arising, abiding and destruction.

They clearly see that the three existences arise from the habitual tendencies of the dysfunction of proliferation from beginningless time. Because they bring to mind the nonappearance and nonarising of the buddhas, they come to understand the realization of the noble ones' Dharma, realized by each for oneself, and so they gain mastery over their own mind. Because they come to understand spontaneous conduct, they will put forth emanated forms, like a jewel of many colors, extremely subtle and adapted to the minds of sentient beings. And by entering into mind-only, they will firmly establish the links that join the successive grounds. Therefore, Mahāmati, bodhisattva mahāsattvas should become skilled in their own established tenets.

Then Mahāmati again made a request: "Blessed One, please explain the flower of the Dharma discourse, the heart of the teachings of all the buddhas. It concerns mind, mentation and consciousness, the five dharmas, and the characteristics of the natures, which the buddhas and bodhisattvas have followed. It frees one from the domain of what appears as one's own mind, and it demolishes the characteristics of assertion, reasoning and reality. For the sake of the bodhisattvas dwelling in the city of Laṅkā and on Malaya in the ocean, please explain the domain of the all-base consciousness, which is like the waves of the ocean, as the Dharma-body Tathāgata has proclaimed it."

Then the Blessed One spoke thus to the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati: "Mahāmati, eye consciousness arises through four causes. What are the four? They are these: not understanding that what is grasped is the appearance of one's own mind; clinging to the habitual tendencies of form produced by the dysfunction of proliferation from beginningless time; the intrinsic, essential nature of consciousness; and fascination with the characteristics of the various kinds of form. Mahāmati, through these four causes the waves of the engaging consciousnesses arise from the all-base consciousness, which is like a flowing river.

"Mahāmati, what holds for eye consciousness holds also for the atoms of all the sense faculties and for the pores of the skin. Forms of objects appear in a mirror all at once or one after another, and the ocean is stirred when struck by the wind. Just so, Mahāmati, the five groups of consciousness arise as waves on the ocean of mind, stirred by the wind of objects. They have the characteristic of the uninterrupted activity of causes. They are free from being either one or different. They do not understand the intrinsic, essential nature that is closely bound up with the characteristics of action and birth.

"Mahāmati, together with those five groups of consciousness there arises mental consciousness, which is called 'that which understands the characteristic of discriminating causes and objects.' It arises as a self produced from them as its cause. Yet neither they nor it think, 'We arise together, each the cause of the other, through clinging to the conceptuality of what appears as our own mind.' Rather, they engage together in discriminating the objects of cognition, as mutually non-different.

"When they engage in this way, even yogins in meditative equipoise do not know how the habitual tendencies, moving with extreme subtlety, are operating. These yogins think, 'We enter absorption having stopped the consciousnesses.' But they enter absorption while consciousness has not ceased. It has not ceased because the seeds of the habitual tendencies have not ceased. It has ceased in the sense that there is no engaging with or grasping of objects.

"Mahāmati, the moving and engaging of the all-base consciousness is extremely subtle. Apart from the tathāgatas and the bodhisattvas well established on the grounds, it is not easy for others to understand it, for śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas, and practitioners of the non-Buddhist yogas. They cannot readily realize it even by the power of samādhi and wisdom.

"Mahāmati, it is otherwise with yogins who are skilled in the characteristics of the grounds, in wisdom and in primordial wisdom. They have no doubt concerning the analysis of words, and they have accumulated limitless roots of virtue with the Victors. They are free of the proliferation of concepts about what appears as their own mind. They dwell in dense forests, in caves and in woodlands, and they practice superior, middling or lesser yoga. Such yogins are able to see the stream of their own mind's conceptuality as it appears. They are able to receive empowerment from the Victors of limitless fields, and to attain the masteries, the powers, the superknowledges and the samādhis."

Mahāmati, those who honor spiritual friends are able to cross the ocean of existence, the saṃsāra of conceptual thought. That ocean is the domain whose nature is the appearance of their own mind, namely mind, mentation and mental consciousness, and it arises from the causes of karma, craving and ignorance. Therefore, Mahāmati, the yogin should exert himself diligently in relying on spiritual friends.

Then, at that time, the Blessed One spoke these verses:

Just as the ocean's waves,
Arising through the condition of the wind,
Dance and surge forth,
And their stream is never cut off,
So too the flood of the ālaya, the storehouse,
Is constantly stirred by the wind of objects,
And the waves of the various consciousnesses
Dance and surge forth.

Blue and red, and salt,
Butter and milk and sugar,
Astringent fruits and flowers,
Just as the rays of sun and moon,
And the ocean's waves
Are known to be neither one nor different,
So too the seven consciousnesses
Are joined together with mind.

The transformation of the ocean
Is those various waves;
Just so, what is called consciousness:
From the ālaya the various arise.

Mind, mentation and consciousness
Are examined in terms of their characteristics;
The eight are not different in mark;
There is neither basis of characterization nor characteristic.

Just as the ocean and its waves
Are without distinction,
So too with mind and consciousness:
No transformation is to be found.

Mind accumulates karma;
Mentation too accumulates;
Through consciousness one cognizes;
The five conceive what appears.

"In forms of blue and red
The consciousness of human beings appears.
Why are the waves of like nature to mind?
Great Sage, please explain."

"Forms of blue and red
Do not exist in the waves;
For the sake of characteristics, to the childish,
The operation of mind is explained.
In it there is no operation;
One's own mind is free of anything grasped.
Where there is something grasped, there is a grasper too;
Thus it is shown to accord with the waves.
Like body, enjoyments and abode,
The consciousness of human beings appears;
Therefore its operation
Appears like the waves."

"If the ocean, in the form of waves,
Is perceived as dancing,
Then why is the operation of the ālaya
Not likewise perceived by the intellect?"

"Because the childish lack understanding,
The ālaya is like the ocean;
Because it accords with the nature of arising waves,
It is presented through an example."

"Just as for people good, bad and middling
The sun rises alike,
So you, lamp of the world,
Teach the truth to the foolish.
Since you abide fully in the Dharma,
Why do you not speak of suchness?"

"If I were to speak of suchness,
There is no suchness in the mind.
Just as the ocean's waves,
And just as a mirror and a dream,
Appear all at one time,
So too mind, in its own domain,
Since it is not joined with its objects all at once,
Arises operating in sequence.
Consciousness cognizes;
Mentation likewise conceives;
To the five, appearance is manifest;
In meditative equipoise there is no sequence.

Some master painter,
Or a painter's apprentice,
Lays out colors to make a picture;
This teaching too is like that.
There is no picture in the colors,
Nor likewise in the brush or in the dish;
For the sake of guiding beings
A picture is fashioned with colors.
In reality there are no letters;
Teachings are deceptive.
Having taken my stand among phenomena,
I teach reality to yogins.
The reality that is personally realized
Is wholly free of the imagined and of imagining.
This is taught to the heirs of the Victors;
It is not explained to the childish.
Just as various illusions
Appear yet do not exist,
So too, though teachings are various,
Teachings are deceptive.
What is taught to one
Is not fit to be explained to another;
Just as a physician gives
Medicines to each patient individually,
So the Buddha, to beings,
Also teaches mind only.
The domain of personal self-awareness
Of which the protectors speak
Is not the domain of those who conceptualize,
Nor likewise is it that of the śrāvakas."

Furthermore, Mahāmati, a bodhisattva who wishes to understand fully the domain of conceptual thought concerning grasped and grasper, which are appearances of his own mind, should be free of gatherings, bustle, sleep and obscurations. He should be diligent in the practice of not sleeping in the evening, at dawn and at midnight. He should keep apart from non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), from bad treatises and idle talk, and from the characteristics of the vehicles of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. The bodhisattva, the great being, should realize and thoroughly comprehend the characteristic of conceptualizing the appearances of his own mind.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, the bodhisattva, the great being, abiding in the establishment of the characteristics of mind, wisdom and gnosis, should strive in the three characteristics of superior noble gnosis. And what, Mahāmati, are the three characteristics of superior noble gnosis? They are these: the characteristic of non-appearance, the characteristic of the blessing of the own aspirations of all the buddhas, and the characteristic of noble personal gnosis. By comprehending these, the yogin abandons the characteristics of mind, wisdom and gnosis as one would a lame donkey, and, having attained the eighth ground of the heirs of the Victors, strives beyond that in the three characteristics.

Here, Mahāmati, the characteristic of the nonappearance of everything arises from thorough familiarity with the characteristics of the śrāvakas, the pratyekabuddhas, and the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas). Mahāmati, the characteristic of empowerment arises from the empowerment of the past buddhas' own aspirations. Mahāmati, the characteristic of noble wisdom individually realized by oneself arises from the movement of realization that comprehends the buddha ground. That realization comes from not clinging to any of the qualities of a buddha or to any characteristic, and from attaining the body of the illusion-like samādhi. Mahāmati, these are the three characteristics of the noble ones. When the noble ones are endowed with these three noble characteristics, they come to comprehend the domain of their own noble wisdom, individually realized. Therefore, Mahāmati, one should strive at these three characteristics of noble wisdom.

Then the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, empowered by all the buddhas, understood the minds and the intentions of that assembly of bodhisattvas. He then asked the Blessed One (the Buddha) about the Dharma discourse called "Discerning the Reality of Noble Wisdom":

"Blessed One, please explain to me the Dharma discourse on the nature that discerns the reality of noble wisdom, the subject in which the hundred and eight statements are thoroughly distinguished. It is that in which the tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly complete buddhas abide. It teaches the bodhisattva great beings the method of distinguishing the realization of the imagined nature that belongs to particular and general characteristics. Through thoroughly realizing the distinctions of that method, they thoroughly purify the movement of selflessness of persons and of phenomena and become skilled in the grounds. They pass beyond the bliss of the meditative concentration, samādhi, and absorption of all śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas, and non-Buddhists. They enter the domain known by the Tathāgata, which is inconceivable and free from the five dharmas and from the realization of the natures. They come to attain the body of the Tathāgata: the Dharma body of the Tathāgata, intimately united with wisdom and gnosis, brought forth through an illusory domain, which goes to all the buddha fields, to the palace of Tuṣita, and to the abode of Akaniṣṭha."

The Blessed One said: "Mahāmati, there are some here who hold the views of non-Buddhists and cling to nonexistence. They think, 'Because the cause of conceptual mind has been exhausted, there is no intrinsic nature, and so there is no horn on a hare.' Just as the hare has no hare's horn, so too they conceive all phenomena.

"Moreover, Mahāmati, others see the distinctions among the elements, qualities, atoms, substances, shapes, and arrangements. Clinging and attached to the nonexistence of the hare's horn, they conceive, 'The cow's horn exists.'

"Mahāmati, those have fallen into the view of the two extremes, and their minds do not understand mind-only. They conceive by way of the element of their own mind. But, Mahāmati, with regard to body, possessions, abode, and destinies, which are mere conceptualization, the hare's horn, which is free from existence and nonexistence, should not be conceived. Likewise, Mahāmati, all things too are free from existence and nonexistence, and they should not be conceived.

"Mahāmati, some conceive of the hare's horn, which is free from existence and nonexistence, as nonexistent. Since these depend on one another as causes, one should not conceive, 'The hare's horn does not exist.' Mahāmati, when one analyzes down to the subtlest particle, no entity is found. Because it does not exist, the existence of a cow's horn, which lies outside the domain of noble wisdom, is not to be conceived."

Then the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, if, having seen the characteristic of the nonarising of conceptuality, one infers, then in dependence on the nonarising of conceptuality one will say, 'It does not exist.'"

The Blessed One said: "Mahāmati, it is not that it does not exist in dependence on the nonarising of conceptuality. Why is that? Because it is the cause from which conceptuality arises. Mahāmati, conceptuality arises in dependence on its horn. Mahāmati, since conceptuality thus arises in dependence on the horn, it is a dependent cause. And because it is free from being either different or not different, it is not the case that the hare's horn does not exist in dependence on that.

"Mahāmati, if the hare's horn and conceptuality were different, conceptuality would not arise from the horn as its cause. If they are not different, then conceptuality arises from that cause. Yet when the horn is thoroughly analyzed down to the subtlest particle, it is not found. Since conceptuality is not different from the horn, it too would be nonexistent. If neither of the two is an entity, what would depend on what, and what would be held to be so? Mahāmati, if nonexistence does not come about through dependence, then because the causes do not correspond, one cannot conceive, in dependence on the existence of a cow's horn, 'The hare's horn does not exist.' Mahāmati, because the causes are unequal, the existence and nonexistence asserted by those who speak of existence and nonexistence are not established.

"Further, Mahāmati, there are those who hold the views of non-Buddhists, attached to form, cause, and shape, and clinging to them. Unskilled in thoroughly understanding the reality of space, they conceive with a view that distinguishes form as separate from the reality of space. Mahāmati, because space follows upon the arising of forms, space itself is form, and form itself is space. Mahāmati, the distinct kinds of cause of form and space should be understood as being related as what is supported and its support. Mahāmati, when the elements arise, they are distinct from one another through the particularity of their own characteristics, and they do not abide in space either. Yet in them too there is no space."

Mahāmati, in the same way, the horn of a hare is posited in relation to the horn of an ox. Yet if the ox's horn is analyzed into atoms, and the atoms too are broken down, so that it does not remain even in the characteristic of an atom, then in relation to what could the hare's horn be nonexistent? And if it were an entity posited in relation to something else, that too would be of just the same nature.

Then the Blessed One spoke these words to the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati: "Mahāmati, you and the other bodhisattvas should be free from the conceptuality that views the horns of the hare and the ox, space, and form. Mahāmati, you should also become one whose mind understands that conceptuality concerns the appearances of your own mind. Among all the children of the Victor and throughout all the maṇḍalas of buddha-fields, you should teach the yoga of the appearances of one's own mind."

Then, at that time, the Blessed One spoke these verses:

The manifold appearances do not exist;
from appearance, mind arises.
As if it were body, enjoyments, and abode,
the all-base appears to people.

Mind, mentation, and consciousness,
the natures and the five dharmas,
and the two selflessnesses, purified:
these the Guide proclaims.

From mutual relation
arise long and short and the rest.
Existence establishes nonexistence,
and nonexistence establishes existence.

Break matter down into atoms,
and form is not to be imagined.
The establishing of mind-only
is not made clear by those with wrong views.

What the protectors teach
is the domain of individual self-awareness.
It is not the domain of the conceptual-minded,
nor is it that of the śrāvakas.

Then the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, again made a request to the Blessed One, for the sake of purifying the continuum of the appearances of one's own mind: "Blessed One, how is the continuum of the appearances of one's own mind purified? Is it all at once, or by gradual entry?"

The Blessed One said: "Mahāmati, the continuum of the appearances of one's own mind is purified by gradual entry, not all at once. Mahāmati, it is like this: the fruit of the mango tree ripens gradually, not all at once. Mahāmati, in the same way, the continuum of the appearances of beings' own minds is purified gradually, not all at once. Mahāmati, it is like this: a potter makes vessels gradually, not all at once. Mahāmati, in the same way, the Tathāgata purifies the continuum of the appearances of beings' own minds gradually, not all at once. Mahāmati, it is like this: from the great earth, grasses, shrubs, medicinal herbs, and forests grow by gradual entry, not all at once. In the same way, Mahāmati, the Tathāgata purifies the continuum of the appearances of beings' own minds gradually, not all at once. Mahāmati, it is like this: skill in comedy, music, song, the lute, cymbals, and painting comes about gradually, not all at once. In the same way, Mahāmati, the Tathāgata purifies the continuum of the appearances of all beings' own minds gradually, not all at once.

"Mahāmati, it is like this: in a mirror, all the reflections of forms appear all at once, without conceptuality. In the same way, Mahāmati, the Tathāgata purifies all at once the continuum of the appearances of all beings' own minds, domains that are free of conceptuality and without appearance. Mahāmati, it is like this: the orbs of the moon and the sun, with their rays, make manifest all at once everything that appears as form.
