So too is conceiving of things.
What is held to arise
From the coming together of causes and conditions is conceptuality.
Not knowing this way thoroughly,
They wander, dwelling in the three realms of existence.

The Noble Descent into Laṅkā, the fourth section.

Then again the bodhisattva great being Mahāmati asked the Blessed One, "Blessed One, with regard to what did the Blessed One also use the word 'permanent'?"

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, because this delusion appears even to the noble ones as not inverted, the word is applied to delusion. For example, Mahāmati, in the world the unwise grasp in an inverted way a mirage, a whirling firebrand, tangled hairs, a city of the gandharvas, a magical illusion, a dream, a reflection and a phantom figure of a person; but the wise do not, though it is not that these do not appear to them. Mahāmati, the fact that this delusion appears in many forms does not make delusion impermanent. Why is that? Because it is free from both existence and nonexistence.

"And how, Mahāmati, is delusion free from existence and nonexistence? It is like the waves of the ocean and the water of the Ganges, which pretas see and do not see: all fools have various domains of experience. Therefore, Mahāmati, delusion is not an existent thing. Yet since that water appears to others, it is not a nonexistent either. Thus, for the noble ones, delusion is free from both the inverted and the non-inverted. Moreover, Mahāmati, since the characteristic of its marks is not different, delusion is permanent. Mahāmati, although delusion is imagined by conceptuality in many various marks, it does not become different. Hence, Mahāmati, delusion is permanent.

"And how, Mahāmati, is delusion reality? Mahāmati, the noble ones engage this delusion neither with an inverted mind nor with a non-inverted mind. Mahāmati, the noble ones perceive this delusion as nothing other than something ordinary; they do not perceive it as an object of noble wisdom. Mahāmati, 'ordinary' is what fools speak of, not what the noble ones speak of.

"Mahāmati, that delusion, when imagined as inverted or non-inverted, brings about the attainment of two lineages: the lineage of the noble ones, or the lineage of foolish ordinary beings. Mahāmati, the lineage of the noble ones is further divided into three kinds: the division into śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas and buddhas.

"Now, Mahāmati, how is it that fools, by imagining delusion, produce the lineage of the śrāvaka vehicle? Mahāmati, it is like this: when one clings with attachment to particular and general characteristics, one comes to belong to the lineage of the śrāvaka vehicle. In this way, Mahāmati, that delusion brings about the attainment of the lineage of the śrāvaka vehicle.

"Then, Mahāmati, how does imagining that very delusion bring about the attainment of the lineage of the pratyekabuddha vehicle? Mahāmati, it is like this: clinging to the particular and general characteristics of that very delusion, and being free from social bustle, brings about the attainment of the lineage of the pratyekabuddha vehicle.

"Then, Mahāmati, how is it that the wise, by imagining that very delusion, attain the lineage of the buddha vehicle? Mahāmati, it is like this: when, because one understands it to be only an appearance of one's own mind, one imagines it without conceiving of external things as existent or nonexistent, one attains the lineage of the buddha vehicle. Therefore, Mahāmati, this is lineage; this is the meaning of lineage."

Mahāmati, when the childish imagine, with regard to error, the intrinsic nature of the various things and think, "This is just so, and not otherwise," they come to obtain the lineage of the vehicle of saṃsāra. Therefore, Mahāmati, the childish imagine error to be various things. Yet it is neither a thing nor the absence of a thing.

Mahāmati, when that very error is examined, the noble ones' mind, mentation and mental consciousness turn away from phenomena whose nature is the habitual tendencies of negative propensities. Through this, error is called suchness for the noble ones. That, Mahāmati, is why it is said that suchness is free from mind. Mahāmati, to teach this very point I said, up to the words "abandoned even by conceptions, free from all conceptions."

Mahāmati asked, "Blessed One, does error exist or not?"

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, error exists like an illusion, but it does not exist as attachment to characteristics. Mahāmati, if error existed through attachment to characteristics, then, Mahāmati, attachment to things could not be reversed. Mahāmati, this too would then be like the dependent arising, the arising from a cause, that the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) teach."

Mahāmati asked, "Blessed One, if error is like an illusion, then it would become the cause of further error."

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, an illusion does not bring about the faults of negative propensities, and so it is not a cause of error. Mahāmati, as long as it is not imagined, an illusion does not bring about the faults of negative propensities. Mahāmati, an illusion arises through the power of another person's spell, not through the power of the habitual tendencies of the negative propensities of one's own conceptual thought. Therefore it does not bring about faults. Mahāmati, it is merely that the childish, attached to whatever it may be, are deceived by viewing it as a sign. It is not so for the noble ones."

On this it is said:

The noble ones do not see error,
Nor is there anything real at that point either.
For if at that point there were something real,
Error itself would become the real.
Even when all error has been abandoned,
If one still gives rise to signs,
That very thing is one's error,
Like the impurity of cataract.

Further, Mahāmati, it is not by seeing that they share with illusion the property of not being nonexistent that all phenomena are like illusions.

Mahāmati asked, "Blessed One, are all phenomena like illusions through the characteristic of attachment to various illusions, or through the characteristic of attachment to what is false? Blessed One, if all things are like illusions through the characteristic of attachment to various illusions, then, Blessed One, things are not like illusions. Why is that? Because the various characteristics of form are seen to be without cause. Blessed One, there is no cause whatever by which, as with an illusion, various characteristics of form come to appear. Therefore, Blessed One, things are not like illusions by sharing a property with attachment to the characteristics of various illusions."

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, it is true that all phenomena are not like illusions by sharing a property with attachment to the characteristics of various illusions. But, Mahāmati, all phenomena are like illusions by sharing a property with lightning, which is false and swift. Mahāmati, it is like this: a flash of lightning perishes in an instant, so that as soon as it is seen it is gone, and it does not appear to the childish. In the same way, Mahāmati, all things appear because there is no discernment of the general characteristics of one's own conceptual thought, and because of attachment to the characteristics of form."

On this it is said:

By sharing with illusion the property of not being nonexistent,
Things are spoken of as existing.
Because they are like lightning, false and swift,
They are therefore known to be like illusions.

Again Mahāmati asked, "The Blessed One has said that all things are unborn, and also that they are like illusions. Blessed One, if you say this, declaring that things are without arising and are like illusions, will your words not surely fall into the fault of contradicting one another, earlier and later?"

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, when I declare that things are without arising and are like illusions, my words do not fall into the fault of contradicting one another. Why is that? It is because I see arising and non-arising, realize them to be merely the appearance of one's own mind, and see that external things do not arise from the existence or the nonexistence of things. Therefore, Mahāmati, my words do not fall into the fault of contradiction. Mahāmati, all things are called unborn, like illusions, in order to demolish the non-Buddhists' position that things arise from a cause. Mahāmati, the deluded schools of the non-Buddhists hold that things arise from existence or from nonexistence, and not from the condition of attachment to the various conceptions of their own conceptual thought. Mahāmati, as I see it, things arise neither from existence nor from nonexistence. Therefore, Mahāmati, I speak in terms of non-arising.

"Again, Mahāmati, things are taught as existing in order to uphold saṃsāra, to avert the nihilism that says 'they do not exist,' and so that my disciples may uphold the ground of birth through various karmas. By holding to the word 'thing,' saṃsāra is upheld."

Mahāmati, these childish ordinary beings are fixated on the characteristics of wrong views. Because they do not see all phenomena as they truly abide, they might deceive both themselves and others. With that concern, I teach that the intrinsic nature and the characteristics of things are like an illusion, and by teaching this I turn them away from the intrinsic nature and characteristics of things. Childish ordinary beings have minds that have fallen into the characteristics of wrong views. They do not realize that things are merely the appearance of their own mind, and they are fixated on the characteristic of arising from causes, conditions and agency. Because they are foolish in this way, I teach that all phenomena have the intrinsic nature and the characteristics of an illusion and a dream. Now, Mahāmati, seeing all phenomena as they truly abide means entering into the mere appearance of one's own mind.

On this it is said:

Where there is no arising, there is no cause.
Through entities, saṃsāra is wholly gathered.
If one sees it as like what comes from causes and the rest,
one does not conceive in terms of characteristics.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, I set forth the characteristics of the groups of names, phrases and letters. When bodhisattva mahāsattvas fully understand these groups, they do not follow after meanings, phrases and letters. They quickly awaken fully into unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, and then they bring all sentient beings to the same understanding.

Mahāmati, the group of names is this: the entity on which one relies to designate a name. "Group," "entity" and "body" do not differ in meaning. That, Mahāmati, is the group of names.

Mahāmati, the group of phrases is this: the many meanings of a phrase. "True," "certain," "conclusive" and "referent" do not differ in meaning. That, Mahāmati, I have taught as the group of phrases.

Again, Mahāmati, the group of letters is this: that by which names and phrases are made manifest. "Letter," "sign," "mark," "referent" and "designation" do not differ in meaning.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, the group of phrases is this: the completion of a phrase. Mahāmati, a name is this: the distinct names that the letters themselves have, from A up to Ha. Mahāmati, the group of letters is this: short pronunciation, long pronunciation, and the ending of the letters.

Mahāmati, as for the group of phrases: elephants, horses, humans, deer, cattle, buffalo, sheep, livestock and all the others that go upon the ground are given the name "group of phrases."

Again, Mahāmati, as for name and letter: name is so called because the four formless aggregates are expressed by names. Letter is so called because it makes things manifest through its own characteristic. That, Mahāmati, is the characteristic by which names and phrases express things, within the groups of names, phrases and letters. You should become thoroughly familiar with it.

On this it is said:

Attached to the groups of phrases and of letters,
and to names, not knowing how they differ in kind,
the childish are stuck fast,
like a great elephant sunk in the mud.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, in times to come, the wise will put questions to unwise and misguided logicians. They will ask about the way of the two extremes, which is free from the characteristics of the views of oneness, difference, both and neither. Then the logicians, lacking reasoning, grounds and intelligence, will say: "This is not a question; it is not proper." For instance, they will be asked whether form and the like are one with impermanence or different from it. Likewise they will be asked about nirvāṇa in relation to formations, the bearer of marks in relation to the marks, the possessor of qualities in relation to the qualities, the elements and what derives from the elements, seeing in relation to what appears, atoms in relation to particles, and yogins in relation to wisdom. When they are asked about these and other things that have been answered by way of characteristics, in sequence one after another, they will say: "This is to be set aside; the Blessed One did not answer it."

As for those deluded people: because listeners such as these lack understanding, the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas, do not answer the questions that are not to be answered, so as to spare sentient beings occasions for terror. And, Mahāmati, the Tathāgatas did not explain those things in order to undo the views of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas).

Mahāmati, the non-Buddhists speak like this: "This body is one with the life principle," and "The body is one thing and the life principle another," and so on, and they declare that such matters are not to be answered. Mahāmati, leaving questions unanswered belongs to the non-Buddhists, who are confused about causes. It does not belong to my teaching. Mahāmati, in my teaching, those who are free from grasped and grasper do not even have conceptual thought at work. So how could there be any setting aside for them? Mahāmati, setting aside exists for those who are fixated on grasped and grasper, whose minds do not know things to be merely the appearance of their own mind.

Mahāmati, when questions are put to them, the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas, teach the Dharma to sentient beings through four kinds of answer. Mahāmati, what is called "setting aside" I taught on another occasion. Setting aside is for those whose faculties have not fully matured.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, all phenomena are without action and agent. Because there is no agent, they do not arise. Therefore all phenomena are said to be unborn.

Mahāmati, all phenomena are without essential nature. Why is that? Mahāmati, when one examines them with one's own intelligence, no entity holds up under scrutiny as having either a particular or a general characteristic. Therefore all phenomena are said to be without essential nature.

Now, Mahāmati, all phenomena are without taking up and casting off. Why is that? Mahāmati, even if one takes up particular and general characteristics, there is nothing to take up. Even if one casts them off, there is nothing to cast off. For that reason, Mahāmati, all phenomena are without taking up and casting off.

Again, Mahāmati, all phenomena are unceasing. Why is that? Entities have no essential nature and no characteristic, so no phenomenon is ever found. Therefore all phenomena are said to be unceasing.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, all phenomena are impermanent. Why is this said? Because the arising of characteristics is impermanent, all phenomena are called impermanent. Furthermore, Mahāmati, all phenomena are permanent. Why is that? It is this: the arising of characteristics is understood as permanent, because it has the nature of non-arising. Therefore, Mahāmati, all phenomena are called permanent.

On this it is said:

Answers are of four kinds:
the categorical, the counter-question,
the distinguishing, and setting aside;
by these the doctrines of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) are refuted.
That things arise from the existent or the nonexistent
is what the Vaiśeṣikas and Sāṃkhyas say.
All the questions left unanswered
were put forward by those very people.
Even when examined by the intellect,
since no intrinsic nature is found,
they cannot be expressed,
and the absence of essential nature is taught.

Then the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, again addressed the Blessed One: "Blessed One, please explain to me the characteristics of the way in which the courses of stream-enterers, who have entered the stream, are classified. When I and the other bodhisattva great beings are skilled in those characteristics, we will know the characteristics of the means by which one advances, ever higher, to become a once-returner, a non-returner and an arhat, and we will teach the Dharma to beings in just the way that suits each. Then we will purify the two characteristics of selflessness and the two obscurations, each in turn, and come to realize the passing beyond the characteristics of each of the grounds. Having found the inconceivable course, domain and sphere of activity of the Tathāgatas, we will become like a jewel of many forms, the sustenance of all beings, and we will sustain them by enjoying, as we wish, the domain and course of all phenomena."

The Blessed One said: "Therefore, Mahāmati, listen well and keep it well in mind, and I will explain it to you."

"Excellent, Blessed One," replied the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, and he listened to the Blessed One. The Blessed One then said to him:

"Mahāmati, the fruit of stream-entry attained by stream-enterers is classified in these three ways. What are the three? They are the lowest, the middling and the supreme. The lowest, Mahāmati, has at most seven more lives in existence. The middling, Mahāmati, attains complete nirvāṇa after three or five existences. The supreme, Mahāmati, attains complete nirvāṇa in that very life. For these three, Mahāmati, there are three fetters, great, middling and small.

"And what, Mahāmati, are the three fetters? They are the view of the perishable collection, doubt, and clinging to moral discipline and vows as supreme. These, Mahāmati, are the three fetters. By overcoming them in their gradations, one rises ever higher to the arhat's fruit of arhatship.

"Mahāmati, the view of the perishable collection is of two kinds: the innate and the imputed. It is like the imputed nature, which is imputed upon the dependent. For, Mahāmati, on the ground of the very nature of the dependent, various kinds of clinging to the imputed nature arise. Since that nature is characterized by imputation, it is neither existent nor nonexistent, nor both existent and nonexistent. Yet the childish, clinging to the characteristics of various natures like a deer to a mirage, construct concepts. That, Mahāmati, is the stream-enterer's imputed view of the perishable collection, a clinging built up over a long time through ignorance. It is abandoned through non-grasping, because the person is without self.

"Mahāmati, the stream-enterer's innate view of the perishable collection is overcome by seeing this: one's own body and the bodies of others are the same; the four aggregates have the characteristic of being formless; form arises from the elements and from what is derived from the elements; and because the elements have the characteristic of being causes of one another, form does not come into being. Since the stream-enterer sees no view that takes the side of existence or nonexistence, the view of the perishable collection is abandoned. Therefore one who has abandoned the view of the perishable collection in this way does not give rise to attachment. That, Mahāmati, is the characteristic of the view of the perishable collection.

"Mahāmati, the characteristic of doubt is this: because the stream-enterer has seen clearly the dharma of attaining realization, and because he has abandoned the conceptualizing of the two kinds of view of the perishable collection described above, he has no doubt about the teachings, and he does not look to another teacher as pure or impure. That, Mahāmati, is the stream-enterer's characteristic of doubt.

"Mahāmati, how does the stream-enterer not cling to moral discipline as supreme? It is this: because he clearly sees the characteristic of birth as a state of suffering, he does not cling to it. Clinging, Mahāmati, is this: through moral discipline, vows and austerities kept without fail, childish ordinary beings who long for enjoyments and pleasure seek birth in saṃsāra. He does not cling in that way. Not clinging thus, he dedicates his practice to something else, to going on to the distinction of what is realized personally, within oneself. He applies himself to the limbs of moral discipline without conceptualizing them, as though they had the characteristic of undefiled phenomena.

That, Mahāmati, is the characteristic of the stream-enterer with respect to pride in moral discipline and ascetic observances. Mahāmati, in the stream-enterer who has abandoned the three fetters, attachment, aversion, and delusion do not arise.

Mahāmati asked, "The Blessed One has taught attachment in many forms. Which attachment has he abandoned?"

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, there is the attachment of the bodily sense faculty to its objects, born from union with women, which is pleasant in the present but becomes a cause for the arising of suffering in the future. Such attachments, of fondling with fist and palm, of joining the lower bodies, of kissing, of embracing, of smelling, and of glancing from the corner of the eye, do not arise in him. Why is that? Because he has abandoned them by attaining abiding in the bliss of meditative absorption. But he has not abandoned the attachment by which nirvāṇa is to be realized.

"Mahāmati, what is the characteristic of the once-returner? It is this: the conceptuality that views things as signs and takes them as the basis of signs, and that which, although without characteristics, appears as the characteristic of form, arises just once. Having clearly seen the characteristic of the domain of meditative concentration, he comes to this world once, and in order to put an end to suffering he passes into final nirvāṇa. Therefore he is called a once-returner.

"And how, Mahāmati, is one a non-returner? It is this: he does not engage with the existence or nonexistence of the entity that is the characteristic of past, future, and present form, and since the conceptuality of the latent faults of views does not return, he is a non-returner. Because he has abandoned the fetters, he is called a non-returner.

"Mahāmati, the arhat is called an arhat because he has no conceptuality regarding meditative concentration, what is to be meditated upon, meditative absorption, the liberations, the powers, the superknowledges, the afflictions, and suffering."

Mahāmati asked, "The Blessed One has spoken of three kinds of arhat. Blessed One, to which of them is this word 'arhat' applied? Blessed One, is it one who has attained the path of solitary peace? Or one who, having cultivated aspirations for awakening, is bewildered about the roots of virtue? Or is it a magical emanation?"

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, it is the śrāvaka who has attained the path of solitary peace, not the others. Mahāmati, the others, those spoken of because they have practiced the conduct of a bodhisattva, and the magical emanations of the buddhas, through skill in means and preceded by their aspirations, display birth within the circles of the assembly in order to adorn the assemblies of the buddhas.

"Mahāmati, the various other grounds into which conceptuality enters, which are taught, are these: meditative concentration for attaining the fruit, what is to be meditated upon, and seclusion from the meditator. These are taught as characteristics of attaining the fruit through acknowledging them as appearances of one's own mind.

"Furthermore, Mahāmati, if a stream-enterer thinks, 'These are the fetters, and I am not endowed with them,' he falls into duality and into the view of a self, and the fetters remain unabandoned.

"Furthermore, Mahāmati, in order to completely transcend the meditative concentrations, the immeasurables, and the formless realm, one should turn away from the characteristic of the appearance of one's own mind. Since the absorption of the cessation of perception and feeling belongs to mind-only, Mahāmati, it is not possible for even that to pass beyond the continuum of the appearance of one's own mind."

On this it is said:

The meditative concentrations and the immeasurables,
Meditative absorption and the formless states,
And the cessation of perception, without remainder,
Do not exist in mind-only.
The fruit of stream-entry,
And likewise that of the once-returner,
The fruit of the non-returner,
And arhatship too, are in the confusion of mind.
Meditation, the object of meditation, the meditator,
Abandonment, and the seeing of the truths:
Whoever understands these to be mere conception
Is thereby set free.

"Furthermore, Mahāmati, there are two kinds of intellect: the intellect of thorough discernment, and the intellect that posits through clinging to the apprehension of conceptual characteristics. Here, Mahāmati, what is called the intellect of thorough discernment is this: the intellect by which, when one thoroughly examines the intrinsic nature and characteristics of things, nothing is found, for they are free from the four extremes. That is the intellect of thorough discernment.

"Here, Mahāmati, the four extremes are these: being free from oneness, difference, both, and neither, and from existence, nonexistence, permanence, and impermanence, is what is explained as the four extremes. Mahāmati, all phenomena are said to be free from these four extremes. Mahāmati, these four extremes should be applied in the thorough examination of all phenomena.

"And what, Mahāmati, is the intellect that posits through clinging to the apprehension of conceptual characteristics? It is this, Mahāmati: through clinging to the apprehension of conceptual characteristics, one asserts that heat, moisture, motion, and solidity, which have the character of false imagination, are the great elements, and through clinging to reasons, characteristics, and examples, one superimposes by false superimposition. That is the intellect that posits through clinging to the apprehension of conceptual characteristics."

Mahāmati, these are the characteristics of the two kinds of intellect. Endowed with these characteristics of the two kinds of intellect, bodhisattva great beings realize and fully understand the characteristic of the selflessness of phenomena and of persons. Skilled in the ground of practice in which an intellect free of appearances thoroughly discerns, they attain the first ground and enter into a hundred samādhis. Having attained the distinctions of samādhi, they see a hundred buddhas and bodhisattvas. They enter into the prior limit and the final limit of a hundred eons, and they illuminate a hundred fields. Having illuminated a hundred fields, they come to know the procedure of the characteristics of the grounds, each higher than the last. Through the distinctions of their aspirations they display miraculous transformations, and they are empowered with the empowerment of the Cloud of Dharma. Having attained the Tathāgata's ground of personal realization, they become endowed with the qualities that possess the ten culminating states. Absorbed in the bliss of what is personally realized, they are adorned with the various light rays of emanation in order to bring sentient beings to full maturity.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, the bodhisattva great being should become skilled in the great elements and in what is derived from the elements. Mahāmati, if one asks how the bodhisattva great being is skilled in the great elements and in what is derived from the elements: Mahāmati, here the bodhisattva great being trains properly in this, and clearly sees, "That in which the great elements are unborn is the truth. These great elements have never truly come into being." Seeing it clearly in this way, he understands that it is merely the appearance of his own mind, and, because external things have no existence, he clearly sees that this triple realm is without elements and without what is derived from the elements; that it is mere name and conceptuality, mere conceptuality of names and of the appearance of one's own mind; that it is pure in the manner of the four extremes, without self and without what belongs to a self; that it abides just as it is, in the true abode of its own characteristic; and that its own characteristic is established as unborn.

Then, Mahāmati, if one asks how what is derived from the elements comes from the great elements: Mahāmati, it is like this. Mahāmati, the great element conceived as moisture produces the water element, outer and inner. Mahāmati, the great element conceived as heat produces the fire element, outer and inner. Mahāmati, the great element conceived as motion produces the wind element, outer and inner. Mahāmati, the great element of the conceptuality that divides off the distinctions of form produces the earth element, outer and inner, together with space. Through clinging to what is false as true, the assemblage of the five aggregates arises, and the great elements and what is derived from the elements arise. Mahāmati, consciousness too, through the cause of clinging to and desiring words and various objects, comes to link up with rebirth in another destiny. The earth element and what is derived from the elements have the great elements as their cause, but for the great elements there is none. Why is that? Mahāmati, it is like this: because there is no entity, no sign, no characteristic, no shape, no nature of activity, and no combination, the great elements do not arise. What is endowed with entity, sign, characteristic, shape, activity, and combination arises from the coming together of activities, not from what lacks signs. Therefore, Mahāmati, the characteristic of the great elements and of what is derived from the elements is something imagined by the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), not by me.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, I shall teach the intrinsic nature of the aggregates as aggregates, and their characteristics. Mahāmati, there are the five aggregates. If one asks what the five are, they are form, feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness. Among them, Mahāmati, four aggregates are formless: feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness. Mahāmati, form is what is derived from the four great elements, the elements whose characteristics are mutually discordant. Mahāmati, the formless ones cannot be counted as four, just as with space. Mahāmati, it is like this: space is beyond the characteristic of number, yet it is conceived of as "space is one." In the same way, Mahāmati, the aggregates too are beyond the characteristic of number and beyond counting. Although they are free of the four extremes, having abandoned the aspects of existence and nonexistence, the childish speak of them by expressing them in terms of number and counting; but the noble ones do not. Mahāmati, the noble ones designate them as having abandoned oneness and difference, like various illusory forms, and as not being another basis, like dreams and like the persons in reflections. Because of deep delusion regarding the realization of noble wisdom, the conceptuality of aggregates appears. Mahāmati, that is the characteristic of the intrinsic nature of the aggregates as aggregates, and you should reverse that conceptuality. Having reversed it, you should teach the Dharma of utter solitude to the maṇḍala of the assembly in all buddha fields, in order to reverse the views of the non-Buddhists. Mahāmati, by teaching the Dharma of utter solitude, the vision of the selflessness of phenomena becomes pure, and one also enters the ground called Gone Far. Then, having entered the great ground Gone Far, one gains mastery over many samādhis. By attaining the mind-made body, one attains the illusion-like samādhi. By realizing and fully understanding the powers, the superknowledges, and the masteries, one becomes, like the earth, the sustenance of all sentient beings. Mahāmati, it is like this: the earth is the sustenance of all sentient beings, and in the same way the bodhisattva great being too becomes the sustenance of all sentient beings.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, there are four kinds of nirvāṇa. If one asks what the four are, they are these: the nirvāṇa that is the absence of an intrinsic nature in entities; the nirvāṇa that is the absence of entities with various characteristics; the nirvāṇa that is the realization that entities with their own characteristics do not exist; and the nirvāṇa that is the cutting off of the stream of the continuum of the individual and general characteristics of the aggregates. These, Mahāmati, are the nirvāṇas of the tīrthikas; they are not found in my teaching. Mahāmati, in my teaching, it is the turning back of the mental consciousness, together with its conceptuality, that is called nirvāṇa.

Mahāmati asked, "Has the Blessed One not established eight consciousnesses?"

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, I have established them."

Mahāmati asked, "If the Blessed One has established them, how is it that the mental consciousness turns back, but not the seven consciousnesses?"

The Blessed One said, "Because it is their cause and their object, the collection of seven consciousnesses arises. Mahāmati, the mental consciousness arises with attachment to the discrimination of objects, and through habitual tendencies it greatly nourishes the all-base consciousness. Together with manas, through attachment to grasping at 'I' and 'mine,' it proceeds as if conceiving 'I.' The all-base consciousness has become its cause and is its object, and its characteristic is that it is not a separate body. Through attachment to objects that are appearances of one's own mind, a collection of minds arises, each one becoming the cause of the other. Mahāmati, like the waves of the ocean, stirred by the wind of objects that are appearances of one's own mind, they proceed and they cease. Therefore, Mahāmati, through the turning back of the mental consciousness, the seven consciousnesses also turn back."

On this it is said:

I do not pass into nirvāṇa
Through activity, characteristics, or entities.
Through turning back from conceptual consciousness,
I pass into nirvāṇa.

With that as its cause and that as its object,
The continuum of manas is firmly established.
The continua of mind bring it forth,
And consciousness too is firmly established.

Just as, when a great body of water is exhausted,
Waves no longer arise at all,
So too the various consciousnesses,
Once they have ceased, will not arise.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, I shall teach the characteristic of the modes of analysis of the imputed nature, by realizing whose fine distinctions you and the other bodhisattva great beings will abandon conceptuality and imputation; will have a mind that clearly sees the domain of my noble, individual self-realization and the ways of the non-Buddhists; will abandon the conceptuality of grasped and grasper; and will no longer discriminate the various distinct aspects of the dependent characteristic as though they were the imputed nature.

Now, Mahāmati, what is the characteristic of the modes of analysis of the imputed nature? It is this: discrimination of expression, discrimination of what is to be expressed, discrimination of characteristics, discrimination of wealth, discrimination of intrinsic nature, discrimination of cause, discrimination of views, discrimination of reasoning, discrimination of arising, discrimination of non-arising, discrimination of connection, and discrimination of bondage and non-bondage. That, Mahāmati, is the characteristic of the modes of analysis of the imputed nature of expression.

Now, Mahāmati, what is discrimination of expression? It is attachment to various melodies and sweet songs. That, Mahāmati, is discrimination of expression.

Now, Mahāmati, what is discrimination of what is to be expressed? It is the conceptual thought, "There exists some intrinsic nature of an entity to be expressed, an object to be known by noble wisdom and realization, resting on which expression comes to proceed."

Now, Mahāmati, what is discrimination of characteristics? It is to cling, through attachment, to various characteristics in what is to be expressed, which is like a mirage; that is, to conceive of all entities as having the characteristics of heat, moisture, motion, and solidity.

Now, what is discrimination of wealth? It is expression referring to objects such as gold, silver, and the many kinds of precious wealth.

Now, what is discrimination of intrinsic nature? It is to grasp entities as having an essential nature, and to view them with the view of false conceptuality, thinking, "This is just so and not otherwise."

Now, what is discrimination of cause? That by which causes and conditions make arising from the characteristic of a cause appear as existent or nonexistent: that is discrimination of cause.

Now, what is discrimination of views? It is attachment to the conceptuality of non-Buddhists who hold the wrong views of existence, nonexistence, oneness, difference, both, and neither.

Now, what is discrimination of reasoning? It is to argue through reasoning and to teach the characteristics of self and of what belongs to self.

Now, what is discrimination of arising? It is attachment to the arising, through conditions, of entities that exist and that do not exist.

Now, what is discrimination of non-arising? It is the notion that all entities are bodies without a cause that did not arise before, and that, having not come into being, they come about through conditions.

Now, what is discrimination of connection? It is being connected with this and that, like a golden thread.

Now, what is discrimination of bondage and non-bondage? It is like attachment to a cause of binding and to something to be bound, just as a person skilled in means ties a knot in a rope and unties it.

Mahāmati, these are the characteristics of the way the imputed nature is analyzed. All childish, ordinary beings cling to these characteristics of the way the imputed nature is analyzed. Mahāmati, because they cling to existence and nonexistence in the dependent characteristic, they cling to the various characteristics of the imagined, just as one sees various things in the basis of a magical illusion. The childish conceptualize with a mind that sees the illusion as something other. Mahāmati, the variety and the illusion are neither one nor different. If they were different, the variety would not arise from the illusion as its cause. If the variety were not different, there would be no distinction between the illusion and the variety. Since a distinction between them is seen, they are neither one nor different. Therefore, Mahāmati, you and the other bodhisattva great beings should not cling to existence and nonexistence with regard to illusion.

On this it is said:

The mind is bound up with objects;
consciousness engages in conceptual thought.
In the appearanceless and in what is distinct,
wisdom truly enters.

The imputed nature exists;
the dependent does not exist.
What the mistaken imagine appears;
they do not imagine the dependent.

Just as an illusion made up of many parts
is not established,
so too the various signs,
when examined, are not established.

Signs are the very substance of the dispositions of negativity;
bondage arises from the mind.
What the ignorant impute,
they conceptualize as the dependent as well.

This very entity that is imagined
is itself the dependent.
The appearance of the various imaginings
is what is called the dependent.

There are the conventional and the ultimate;
there is no third that arises from causes.
The imagined is explained as the conventional;
its cutting off is the domain of the noble ones.

Just as for the object of a yogin
the variety shines beautifully as one,
yet in it there is no variety,
such is the characteristic of the imagined.

Just as those afflicted with cataracts
imagine variety in visible forms,
while the cataract is neither form nor non-form,
so the wise regard the dependent.

Just as gold that has been purified,
water rid of its turbidity,
and the sky free of clouds,
so purity is fully discerned.

The imagined has no substance;
the dependent exists.
Superimposition and denial,
when examined, are destroyed.

If the imagined has no substance
and yet the dependent truly exists,
then where there is no entity there is an entity,
and an entity arises from no entity.

Abiding in the imputed,
one perceives the dependent.
From the connection of name and sign
the imputed comes to arise.

The imagined is utterly unestablished;
it does not arise from another.
Then the ultimate nature
is known to be utterly pure.

The imputed is tenfold;
the dependent is sixfold.
Suchness is to be known by individual self-awareness;
therefore there is no distinction.

The five dharmas are reality itself,
and so likewise are the three natures.
If a yogin meditates on them,
he does not pass beyond suchness.

The signs are the dependent;
whatever is name, that is the imagined.
The imputed and the signs
arise from the dependent.

When the intellect examines,
there is no dependent and no imagined either.
If there is no perfected entity,
what is it that the intellect examines?

If there is a perfected entity
that is free of existence and nonexistence,
how could an entity
released from existence and nonexistence become two?

In the imputed nature
the two natures are established.
The imagined appear as variety;
the pure is the domain of the noble ones.

The appearance of the various imaginings
is called the dependent.
If it is imagined in any other way,
one comes to abide in the doctrine of the non-Buddhists.

Conceptual thought is called the imagined;
because it is seen, it arises from causes.
Freedom from the two kinds of conceptuality
is itself the perfected.

Then Mahāmati again asked, "Blessed One, please explain the characteristic of the realization of the noble individual self-wisdom and of the one vehicle, the characteristic through which I and the other bodhisattva great beings may become skilled in the noble individual self-wisdom and in the one vehicle, and may come to rely on no one else with regard to all the qualities of a buddha."

The Blessed One said, "In that case, Mahāmati, listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain it to you."

Saying, "Excellent, Blessed One," the bodhisattva great being Mahāmati listened to the Blessed One, and the Blessed One spoke as follows:

"Mahāmati, a bodhisattva great being goes alone into solitude. Without conceptualizing in terms of valid cognition and trustworthy scripture, he examines with his own individual intellect, relying on no one else. Having abandoned views and conceptuality, he strives to enter ever higher into the ground of the Tathāgata. Mahāmati, that is the characteristic of realizing the individual noble wisdom.

"What, then, is the characteristic of realizing the Great Vehicle? It is this: it is called the one vehicle because one knows and understands the path of the one vehicle. And what is it to know and understand the path of the one vehicle? It is this: because, through abiding in reality just as it is, the conceptuality of grasped and grasper does not arise, the one vehicle has been understood. Mahāmati, this understanding of the one vehicle has never before been attained, apart from me, by other non-Buddhists, by śrāvakas, by pratyekabuddhas, or by Brahmā and other teachers. Therefore, Mahāmati, it is called the one vehicle."

Mahāmati asked, "Why has the Blessed One taught three vehicles and not taught a single vehicle?"

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, it is like this. All śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas are liberated through the Tathāgata's discipline and through the instruction in the yoga of utter solitude, not by their own power. Mahāmati, because they are not of a nature to attain final nirvāṇa by themselves, the single vehicle has not been taught to all the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas.

"Furthermore, Mahāmati, all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas have not abandoned the obscuration to the knowable or the habitual tendencies of karma. Because of this they have not realized the selflessness of phenomena, and they have not attained the inconceivable death by transformation. Therefore the three vehicles are taught to the śrāvakas, and not the single vehicle.

"Mahāmati, when they have abandoned all the habitual tendencies of faults and have realized the selflessness of phenomena, they become free of the faults of habitual tendencies and of the intoxication of samādhi. Then they awaken once more from the undefiled realm, and having completed the accumulations, both worldly and transcendent, that belong to the undefiled realm, they come to attain mastery over the inconceivable Dharma body."

On this it is said:

"The vehicle of the gods and the vehicle of Brahmā,
And likewise that of the śrāvakas,
Those of the Tathāgatas and the pratyekabuddhas:
These vehicles I have taught.
As long as beings go on entering,
There is no end to the vehicles.
When the mind is thoroughly transformed,
There is no vehicle, and no one who goes.
Though there is no setting up of vehicles,
In order to lead sentient beings
I have taught the various vehicles;
I have taught the single vehicle.
Likewise the three liberations,
And the selflessness of phenomena itself,
The wisdom of sameness, and the afflictions:
Through liberation these are abandoned.
Just as logs upon the ocean
Are tossed about by the waves,
So too the deluded śrāvakas
Are tossed about by characteristics.
Though free of the manifest afflictions,
They are bound up with the habitual tendencies of affliction.
Drunk with the intoxication of samādhi,
They dwell within the undefiled realm.
For them there is no reaching the end,
Yet they do not turn back from there again.
Having attained the body of samādhi,
They do not awaken for eons.
Just as a drunken man,
Once free of liquor, becomes sober,
So they too will then
Attain my Dharma body."

This concludes the second chapter, called The Compendium of All Phenomena, from the Laṅkāvatāra in Thirty-Six Thousand Lines.

Then again the Blessed One spoke these words to the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati: "Mahāmati, I shall teach the characteristics that distinguish the realization of the mind-made body. Listen very well and bear it in mind, and I will explain it to you."

Saying, "Excellent, Blessed One," the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati listened to the Blessed One, and the Blessed One spoke to him these words: "Mahāmati, the mind-made body is of three kinds. What are the three? They are the mind-made body that is absorbed in the bliss of samādhi, the mind-made body that has come to understand the realization of the essential nature of phenomena, and the mind-made body of the activity of formations born together with one's class. Yogins attain these by fully knowing the characteristics of the grounds, higher and higher from the first ground onward.

"Among these, Mahāmati, what is the mind-made body that is absorbed in the bliss of samādhi? On the third, fourth, and fifth grounds, by practicing the many aspects of one's own mind in solitude, the mentation absorbed in bliss, which has the character of the waves of consciousness entering the ocean of mind, no longer arises. Through fully knowing the existence and nonexistence of entities in the objects that appear as one's own mind, it is called a mind-made body.

"Among these, what is the mind-made body that has come to understand the realization of the essential nature of phenomena? On the eighth ground, through understanding the thorough discernment that phenomena such as illusions do not appear, the basis of the mind is thoroughly transformed. One fully attains the illusion-like samādhi and fully attains the other doors of samādhi. The flowers of the signs, the masteries, and the many superknowledges blossom. Swift as thought, like an illusion, a dream, or a reflection, it is not made of the elements, yet it resembles the elements and what derives from them. Gathering together all the various limbs of form, it follows the circles of the assemblies in all the buddha fields. Because it has come to understand the realization of the essential nature of phenomena, that body is called a mind-made body.

"Among these, what is the mind-made body of the activity of formations born together with one's class? Through understanding the blissful character of the individual self-knowing of all the qualities of a buddha, it is called the mind-made body of the activity of formations born together with one's class.

"Mahāmati, you should exert yourself in this knowledge that thoroughly discerns the characteristics of the three bodies."

On this it is said:

"My Great Vehicle is not a vehicle;
It has no letters, and no sound either;
There are no truths, there is no liberation,
No appearance, and no sphere of activity.
Yet the Great Vehicle is a vehicle:
It has mastery over samādhi,
And the various mind-made bodies
Are adorned with the flowers of mastery."

Then again the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati spoke these words to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, what are the five acts of immediate retribution spoken of by the Blessed One, by committing which a son or daughter of good family goes to the Avīci hell?"

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, then listen very well and bear it in mind, and I will explain it to you."

Saying, "Excellent, Blessed One," the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati listened to the Blessed One, and the Blessed One spoke these words: "Mahāmati, what are the five acts of immediate retribution? They are killing one's father, one's mother, or an arhat; dividing the Saṅgha; and drawing the blood of the Tathāgata with malicious intent."

Now, Mahāmati, what is the mother of beings? Craving, which brings renewed existence and is accompanied by delight and attachment, stands as the mother. Ignorance is like the father, because it gives rise to the village of the sense sources. If these two, father and mother, are cut off entirely at the root, that is the killing of father and mother. And what is the killing of an arhat? The latent tendencies are like enemies, and like rat poison they have the nature of rising up from within. If they are utterly destroyed, that is the killing of an arhat. And what is the splitting of the Saṅgha? The aggregates form an assembly whose characteristics are at odds with one another. When that assembly is utterly destroyed at the root, it is called the splitting of the Saṅgha.

Mahāmati, consider the eight assemblies of consciousness, which take particular and general characteristics, and the external world, to be mere appearances of one's own mind. If they are utterly destroyed by the uncontaminated malice of the three liberations, that is the drawing of blood with malice from the buddha who is consciousness, and it is called committing the deeds of immediate consequence. Mahāmati, if a son of good family or a daughter of good family commits these five deeds of immediate consequence, that one comes to clear realization. Such a one is a doer of the deeds of immediate consequence.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, I shall explain to you the external deeds of immediate consequence, so that through this explanation you and the other bodhisattva great beings will not fall into deep confusion in the future. They are the deeds of immediate consequence spoken of in the expression of the teaching. One who has committed them does not come to clear realization through any of the three liberations, except for realization through the blessing of emanation.

Mahāmati, a śrāvaka who is an emanation, through the blessing of a bodhisattva or the blessing of a tathāgata, may have someone in mind who has committed a deed of immediate consequence and become remorseful. He thinks, "I will make him enthusiastic once more, to turn back his remorse and so that he does not dwell in remorse at having abandoned his effort." With that aim, this emanated śrāvaka displays clear realization through the blessing of emanation.

Mahāmati, for one who commits the deeds of immediate consequence there is no certainty whatever of clear realization. The one exception is this: he may understand body, enjoyments and abode as mere appearances of his own mind, and see that the conceptual thought which conceives them, grasping at self and mine, is utterly void. Then, if he perchance meets a spiritual friend, he will be freed from the faults of his own conceptual thought in another rebirth.

On this it is said:

Craving is declared to be the mother,
And ignorance is known as the father.
Because consciousness cognizes objects,
It in turn is taught to be the buddha.
The latent tendencies are the arhat,
The five aggregates assembled are the Saṅgha.
Because these are cut off with no interval between,
That is the deed of immediate consequence.

Then Mahāmati again requested, "Blessed One, please explain to me the buddhahood of the buddhas. Blessed One, in what way is it the buddhahood of the buddhas?"

The Blessed One said, "It comes from realizing the selflessness of phenomena and of persons, from thoroughly knowing and comprehending the two obscurations, from attaining the two kinds of death and transference, and from abandoning the two afflictions. Mahāmati, that is the buddhahood of the buddha blessed ones. Mahāmati, by comprehending these very dharmas, śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas also attain buddhahood. Therefore, Mahāmati, I teach a single vehicle."

On this it is said:

The two selflessnesses, the afflictions,
And likewise the two kinds of obscuration,
And by attaining the death and transference
Of inconceivable transformation, one is a tathāgata.

The Noble Descent into Laṅkā, fifth fascicle.

Then once again the bodhisattva great being Mahāmati spoke to the Blessed One: "The Blessed One, entering the midst of the assembly, said: 'All the buddhas who have passed away are I myself. In the many different births of the birth stories, too, I myself was, at those various times, King Māndhātṛ, the elephant, Śuka, Vyāsa, Indra, Sunetra and the others.' With what intention did the Blessed One speak, when he told the hundred thousand birth stories?"

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, when the tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly complete buddhas, enter the midst of the assembly, they have four kinds of sameness in mind when they utter the words, 'At that time, in that age, I myself was Krakucchanda, Kanakamuni and Kāśyapa.' And what four kinds of sameness do they have in mind? Sameness of letters, sameness of speech, sameness of Dharma, and sameness of body. Mahāmati, the tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly complete buddhas, have these four kinds of sameness in mind when they enter the midst of the assembly and utter those words.

"Now, Mahāmati, what is sameness of letters? The very letters that make my name, Buddha, are the same letters for those buddha blessed ones. Mahāmati, those letters do not differ in their nature as letters. That, Mahāmati, is sameness of letters.

"Now, Mahāmati, what is the sameness of speech of the tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly complete buddhas? The articulation of my speech issues forth with the sixty-four aspects of Brahmā's melodious voice. Mahāmati, the articulation of the speech of those tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly complete buddhas likewise issues forth with the sixty-four aspects of Brahmā's melodious voice. In their nature as the voice of the kalaviṅka bird and the melodious voice of Brahmā, there is no difference between them, nothing more and nothing less.

"And what is sameness of body? I and those tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly complete buddhas are the same and without distinction in the Dharma body, in form, and in the body adorned with the excellent major and minor marks. The one exception is that, for the sake of training beings, the tathāgatas display various forms in the various destinies of beings."

Now, Mahāmati, what is sameness of Dharma? It is this: those buddhas and I have realized the thirty-seven factors of awakening. Mahāmati, it is with these four kinds of sameness in mind that the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas, make such statements when they go among the assembly. On this it is said:

"Kāśyapa, Krakucchanda,
and Kanakamuni too were I."
Because we are the same in sameness,
I have spoken thus to the Victor's heirs.

Again Mahāmati asked: "The Blessed One has said: 'From the night the Tathāgata attains perfect and complete awakening until the night he passes into final nirvāṇa, in all that time the Tathāgata has not spoken a single syllable, does not speak, and will not speak, for non-speech is the speech of the Buddha.' With what in mind did the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly and completely awakened buddha, say that non-speech is the speech of the Buddha?"

The Blessed One replied: "Mahāmati, I said this with two dharmas in mind. What are the two? They are the dharmatā realized by each for oneself, and the dharmatā of the ancient way. Mahāmati, it is with these two dharmas in mind that I said this.

"What is meant by the dharmatā realized by each for oneself? It is this: the domain of individual self-awareness that those Tathāgatas realized, free from the conceptuality of words and released from the domain of letters and of duality, I too have realized, with nothing added and nothing missing.

"And what is the dharmatā of the ancient way? Mahāmati, it is this: this path of the ancient nature of things is like a mine of gold, silver, and pearls. Mahāmati, the abiding of the realm of phenomena remains whether Tathāgatas appear or do not appear. Its dharmatā, its abiding as Dharma, its immutability as Dharma, continue on like the road to an ancient city.

"Suppose, Mahāmati, that a certain person wandering in the wilderness saw an ancient city entered by a road that does not stray, and went to that city, entered it, dwelt in the city, and enjoyed the activities and pleasures of the city. Mahāmati, what do you think of that? Were the road by which he entered the city and the many features of the city made by that person?"

Mahāmati replied, "No, Blessed One."

The Blessed One said: "Just so, Mahāmati, the dharmatā that I and those Tathāgatas have realized, its abiding as Dharma, its immutability as Dharma, its suchness, its reality, its truth, simply remain. Therefore, for that reason, Mahāmati, I said: 'From the night the Tathāgata attains perfect and complete awakening until the night he passes into final nirvāṇa, in all that time the Tathāgata has not spoken a single syllable and will not speak.'" On this it is said:

From the night on which I realized
to that on which I passed into nirvāṇa,
in all the time between,
I taught nothing at all.
With the personally realized Dharma and the ancient way
in mind, I have proclaimed this.
Those buddhas and I
make not the slightest difference.

Then again the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, made this request to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, please explain the characteristics of the existence and nonexistence of all phenomena, so that I and the other bodhisattva great beings may abandon existence and nonexistence and swiftly awaken fully to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening."

The Blessed One said: "Then, Mahāmati, listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain it to you."

"Excellent, Blessed One," replied the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, and he listened to the Blessed One. Then the Blessed One spoke to him as follows:

"Mahāmati, these worlds rest on two things: they rest on existence and they rest on nonexistence. Having fallen into views that hold to arising and to non-arising, they conceive of an escape where there is no escape.

"Now, Mahāmati, how does the world rest on existence? It is like this: in the world, things arise from causes and conditions that exist, not from ones that do not exist. What exists arises as arising; what does not exist does not. Mahāmati, those who speak thus of things as existing do not speak of causes and conditions and the world as existing from what is causeless.

"And how, Mahāmati, does one rest on nonexistence? It is like this: having accepted attachment, aversion, and delusion, one afterwards conceives of attachment, aversion, and delusion as nonexistent. Mahāmati, there is the one who does not accept things as existent because the characteristics of things are utterly isolated, and who, because the characteristics of things are utterly isolated, does not accept attachment, aversion, and delusion in buddhas, śrāvakas, and pratyekabuddhas. Mahāmati, of these, the one called 'existent' and the one called 'nonexistent', which is the destroyer?"

Mahāmati replied, "Blessed One, it is the one who accepted attachment, aversion, and delusion and later does not accept them."

The Blessed One said: "Excellent, Mahāmati, excellent! Mahāmati, that you have understood it so is excellent of you. Mahāmati, it is not merely the nonexistence of attachment, aversion, and delusion that such a one destroys; he also destroys the śrāvakas, the pratyekabuddhas, and the buddhas. Why is that? Because the afflictions are not apprehended as one or as different, or as internal or external. Mahāmati, because attachment, aversion, and delusion have no body, they are not apprehended as internal or external. Mahāmati, by not accepting the entities of attachment, aversion, and delusion, one also destroys the buddhas, the śrāvakas, and the pratyekabuddhas."

"Because there is nothing to be bound and no cause of bondage, those buddhas, śrāvakas, and pratyekabuddhas are liberated by their very nature. Mahāmati, if there were something to be bound, there would also be bondage and a cause of bondage. Mahāmati, one who speaks in this way is also a destroyer. Mahāmati, that is the characteristic of nonexistence. Mahāmati, it was with this in mind that I said: 'Better a view of a person as large as Mount Meru than the view of emptiness held by one who is proud about existence and nonexistence.' Mahāmati, one who is proud about nonexistence is also a destroyer.

"A mind that has fallen into particular and general characteristics does not fully understand them to be mere appearances of one's own mind. Not understanding this, it views external things as impermanent, and so it imagines the aggregates, elements, and sense sources as divided, split apart moment after moment. If it conceives of what is beyond letters as arising and ceasing through the continuity of a series, that too is destructive."

On this it is said:

"Whatever lies in the domain of mind
Is the two extremes, existence and nonexistence.
When that domain has been wholly abandoned,
Mind too will truly cease.

Because there is no grasping at objects,
It is not that there is no cessation.
Just like the domain of the noble ones,
There is the abode of suchness.

That through conditions, out of existence and nonexistence,
What had not arisen is born,
And having arisen then perishes:
That has no place in my teaching.

If neither buddhas nor non-Buddhists,
Neither I nor any others,
Have established existence through conditions,
How then could there be nonexistence?

Who has ever established existents,
That they should become nonexistent through conditions?
Those who assert arising, through their wrong view,
Imagine views of existence and nonexistence.

Those for whom nothing whatever arises
And nothing whatever ceases,
Since they see the world as void,
Do not grasp at existence and nonexistence."

Then once again the bodhisattva great being Mahāmati addressed the Blessed One: "Blessed One, there is a thorough realization of the distinctions among the different characteristics of the way of the established truth (siddhānta). Through it, I and the other bodhisattva great beings will understand the realization of the characteristics of the way of the established truth, will quickly awaken fully into unsurpassed, perfect, and complete awakening, and will not fall under the sway of speculators, of misguided non-Buddhists, or of any māras. Please explain to me the characteristics of the way of the established truth. Sugata, please explain it to me. Tathāgata, arhat, perfectly and completely awakened Buddha, supreme among speakers, please explain it to me."

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, therefore listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain it to you."

"Excellent, Blessed One," he replied, and the bodhisattva great being Mahāmati listened to the Blessed One. The Blessed One then spoke to him as follows:

"Mahāmati, the characteristics of the way of the established truth of all śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas, and bodhisattvas are of two kinds, namely the way of the established truth and the way of teaching.

"Mahāmati, the way of the established truth is this: it is the characteristic of the distinction of personal self-awareness. It brings about the attainment of the realization of the undefiled realm, which is free of verbal conceptualization and of letters. It is the intrinsic characteristic of personal realization, which has left behind all non-Buddhists and māras. Having vanquished all those speculators, non-Buddhists, and māras, personal self-awareness shines in its beauty. Mahāmati, that is the characteristic of the way of the established truth.

"What, then, is the way of teaching? It is this: the ninefold teaching expounded in its variety. Abandoning the positions of oneness and difference, of existence and nonexistence, and preceded by the procedures of skillful means, the teaching enters into all beings: whatever each one is inclined toward, that is what is taught to them. Mahāmati, that is the characteristic of the way of teaching, and you and the other bodhisattva great beings should strive in it."

On this it is said:

"The established truth and the ways
Are personal self-awareness and teaching.
Those who see with knowledge of their difference
Do not fall under the power of speculation.

If there is no true thing at all
Such as fools imagine,
How is it that the speculators
Do not hold liberation to be the absence of things?

Even when they look at conditioned things one by one
As bound up with arising and perishing,
The two views grow ever greater,
And, being inverted, they do not see.

When one sees the worlds as imagined, as illusions,
And as like the trunk of a plantain tree,
Nirvāṇa, in which mentation is abandoned,
This alone is the truth.

There is no attachment and no aversion,
There is no delusion and no person.
The aggregates that arise from craving
Exist only as a dream."

Then once again the bodhisattva great being Mahāmati addressed the Blessed One: "Blessed One, please explain to me the characteristics of false imagination. Sugata, please explain them to me. Blessed One, how, as what, through what, and in whom does false imagination come to arise? Blessed One, when one speaks of false imagination as 'false imagination,' Blessed One, what is it that is called false imagination? Through examining what does it become false imagination?"

The Blessed One said, "Excellent, excellent, Mahāmati! In asking about such a matter, Mahāmati, you have set out for the benefit of many people, for the happiness of many people, out of compassion for the world, and for the welfare, benefit, and happiness of gods, humans, and the great multitude of beings. Excellent! Mahāmati, therefore listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain it to you."

"Excellent, Blessed One," he replied, and the bodhisattva great being Mahāmati listened to the Blessed One. The Blessed One then spoke to him as follows:

"Mahāmati, people's conceptuality arises because they are attached to what is falsely imagined as a diverse variety of distinct objects. Mahāmati, there are those who are attached to and cling to the grasped and the grasper, whose understanding has not realized that things are merely appearances of their own mind. And, Mahāmati, there are those who have fallen to the side of views of existence and nonexistence, whose habitual tendencies of discriminating conceptuality toward the views of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) have grown very strong. In these beings many minds and mental factors arise, known as 'conceptuality,' because they are attached to perceiving diverse external objects and attached to self and what belongs to self."

Mahāmati said: "Blessed One, suppose people's conceptuality arises because they are attached to falsely imagining a diverse variety of distinct objects. Suppose those who have fallen into views on the side of existence and nonexistence, into the grasped and the grasper, whose habitual tendencies of discriminating the views of the tīrthikas have grown strong, are attached to perceiving diverse external objects. Suppose they do not know that things are merely appearances of their own mind, and so, attached to diverse things as existent and nonexistent, many minds and mental factors arise in them, known as 'conceptuality.' Blessed One, this conceptuality is turned away from the characteristic of the views that have fallen to the side of the existence and nonexistence of the characteristics of diverse external objects, and that are devoid of entity and nonentity. Likewise, Blessed One, the ultimate too is turned away from the characteristics of valid cognition, the sense faculties, parts, examples, and reasons. Blessed One, how then is it that in the one case conceptuality arises through discriminating attachment to diverse things of unreal objects as existent and nonexistent, while in the case of attachment to and conceptualizing of the characteristic of the ultimate, conceptuality does not arise? Blessed One, if you say that it arises in the one case and does not arise in the other, you will be propounding attachment to the side of existence and nonexistence, and a cause that is not consistent. If you say that the view of false conceptuality arises, then, just as a single form is made up out of the diverse distinct illusory limbs of a person, so diverse characteristics are imagined through discrimination. And because this turns away from conceiving entity and nonentity, your teaching would fall into the view and the outlook of the worldly Lokāyatas."

The Blessed One said: "Mahāmati, conceptuality neither arises nor ceases. Why is that? It is because conceptuality does not arise from existence and nonexistence, because the things that appear as external are not things, and because one understands them as merely appearances of one's own mind. So, Mahāmati, there is neither arising nor ceasing of conceptuality. Mahāmati, nevertheless, I have taught that conceptuality arises, preceded by what has come down from the past, because fools imagine through the conceptuality of their own diverse minds and are attached to the characteristics of diverse things. I taught it to show how these foolish ordinary beings come to comprehend things as merely their own conceptualizing mind. They turn their views away from attachment to self and what belongs to self, and turn away from the faults of cause, condition, and result. By realizing things as merely appearances of their own mind, their mind and outlook are thoroughly transformed. Having gained understanding of all the grounds, they will attain the domain of personal self-awareness of me, the Tathāgata, the turning away of the five dharmas, of natures, of things, of views, and of conceptuality. Therefore, Mahāmati, I have said this: conceptuality toward illusion arises as attachment to diverse unreal objects, and by fully knowing the objects of one's own diverse conceptuality as they truly are, one becomes liberated."

On this it is said:

Those who say the world arises
Through causes and through conditions
Are bound up with the four extremes;
They do not know my way.

From causes and from conditions
The world is not born as existent or nonexistent.
Since it is in no way existent or nonexistent,
How can fools imagine it so?

Not existent, not nonexistent, not both existent and nonexistent:
When the world is seen in this way,
Then the mind too turns away,
And selflessness is realized.

Because they arise from conditions,
All things are unborn.
All conditions are results,
And from a result no existence is born.

Since a result would then be twofold,
No result is born from a result.
Because it would become twofold,
No resultant thing is found.

When conditioned things are seen
Without perceiving and perceived,
That is the mind-only of no mind;
This too I have taught as mind-only.

"Only" is the shape of essential nature,
Free of conditions and of things;
The ultimate thing, the supreme brahman:
This I have taught as "only."

The self is in truth imputed;
It is not a substantially existent thing.
Likewise the aggregate-ness of the aggregates
Is imputed and without substance.

Sameness is of four kinds:
Arising from marks, from causes, and from things,
And the sameness of selflessness,
The fourth, belonging to the yoga of yogins.

Turning away all views,
Free of conceiving and imagining,
Without object and without birth:
This too I have taught as mind-only.

Neither nonentity nor non-entity,
Entirely free of entity and nonentity,
And likewise liberation from mind:
This too I have taught as mind-only.

Suchness and the limit of emptiness,
Nirvāṇa and the realm of phenomena,
And the diverse mind-made bodies:
These too I have taught as mind-only.

Bound up with the habitual tendencies of conceptuality,
Arising from the diverse mind,
It appears to people as external:
This is the mind-only of the worldly.

What appears externally does not exist;
It is mind that appears as diverse things,
Appearing like body, enjoyments, and abode:
This too I have taught as mind-only.

Then again the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, said this to the Blessed One:

"The Blessed One has said that bodhisattva great beings and others should not grasp the meaning according to the words. How, then, Blessed One, does a bodhisattva great being avoid grasping the meaning according to the words? What is the word, and what is the meaning?"

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, then listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain it to you."

Saying, "Excellent, Blessed One," the bodhisattva great being Mahāmati listened to the Blessed One once more, and the Blessed One spoke to him thus:

"Now, Mahāmati, what is the word? It is conceptuality bound up with words and letters. That which comes from the joining of teeth, jaws, palate, tongue and lips, and which is born, as people speak with one another, from the cause of the habitual tendencies of conceptuality, is called the word.

"Now, Mahāmati, what is the meaning? A bodhisattva great being goes alone into solitude. By the wisdom that comes from hearing, reflection and meditation, he uses his own understanding to examine thoroughly the path that leads to the city of nirvāṇa. That path is the domain of the noble ones' self-realized knowledge, and it is preceded by the transformation of the basis of habitual tendencies. He also examines the distinctions among the grounds and the other stations of the grounds, and how one enters into the characteristic of meaning. When he examines these things thoroughly, he is skilled in meaning.

"Moreover, Mahāmati, a bodhisattva great being who is skilled in word and meaning rightly sees that word and meaning, or meaning and word, are neither one nor different. Mahāmati, if word and meaning were different, the word could not be the cause that makes the meaning clear. Yet the word gives access to that meaning, as a lamp does to one's valuables. Mahāmati, it is like a certain person who takes up a lamp and looks at his valuables, saying, 'Here in this place is my wealth, of such a kind.' Just so, Mahāmati, by the lamp of the word, which is conceptualized in speech, bodhisattva great beings gain access to the object of self-realized meaning, which is free from the conceptuality of words.

"Moreover, Mahāmati, consider non-arising, non-cessation, being by nature completely in nirvāṇa, the three vehicles, the one vehicle, the five dharmas, mind, intrinsic nature and the rest. Some cling to these on the basis of clinging to the words just as they are. Because they cling in this way, they fall into views of superimposition and denial, like the conceptuality that sees various illusions. For they conceptualize in one way what abides in another. Mahāmati, various illusions are seen as other than they are. In the same way the childish conceptualize things as other than they are, but the noble ones do not."

On this it is said:

Having conceived of words just as they are,
They superimpose even upon the nature of reality.
Because they superimpose upon it,
Those beings fall into the abodes of hell.

There is no self in the aggregates,
Nor are the aggregates in the self.
They do not exist as they are conceived,
Yet neither are they nonexistent.

The childish conceive
That all things exist.
If things existed just as they are seen,
Then everyone would see the truth.

Since all phenomena are nonexistent,
There is neither affliction nor purity.
They do not exist as they are seen,
Yet neither are they nonexistent.

"Moreover, Mahāmati, I shall explain to you the characteristics of wisdom and consciousness. By thoroughly understanding their distinct aspects, you and the other bodhisattva great beings will comprehend the characteristics of wisdom and consciousness, and you will quickly and fully awaken to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

"Now, Mahāmati, wisdom is of three kinds: worldly, supramundane, and highest supramundane. Among these, what arises and then perishes is consciousness. What neither arises nor perishes is wisdom.

"Moreover, Mahāmati, consciousness falls into signs and signlessness. It falls into existence and nonexistence, and it is born from the cause of various characteristics. The characteristic that wholly transcends signs and signlessness is wisdom.

"Moreover, Mahāmati, the characteristic of accumulating is consciousness, and the characteristic of diminishing is wisdom.

"Now, the three kinds are these: the kind that understands particular and general characteristics, the kind that understands arising and perishing, and the kind that understands non-arising and non-cessation.

"Worldly wisdom belongs to all tīrthikas and all childish ordinary beings, who cling to the positions of existence and nonexistence.

"Supramundane wisdom belongs to all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, who cling to an outlook that falls into particular and general characteristics.

"The highest supramundane wisdom is that of the buddhas and bodhisattvas. It thoroughly discerns the dharma that is without appearance. It sees non-arising and non-cessation, and it is free from the positions of existence and nonexistence. It arises because they realize the selflessness of the ground of the Tathāgata.

"Moreover, Mahāmati, the characteristic of non-attachment is wisdom, and the characteristic of attachment to various objects is consciousness.

"Moreover, Mahāmati, the characteristic of arising through the activity in which three things come together is consciousness. The characteristic of an essential nature that does not arise in this way is wisdom.
